NATHAN MCKNIGHT -- UNFINISHED PIECES AND NOTES

TITLE: Hatchlings (unfinished novel)

(c) 2024 by Nathan McKnight

Prologue: The Egg-Singer 
Prologue:  Home
[Luce & Ivan in prison. Luce telling story for 97th time of how he got captured… knocking on the door… Ivan laughs at the guy getting his head shot off every time. Ivan gets his head shot off & luce is told he’s going home. (Russian prisoners liquidated, as well as russian Sakhas. Wealthy Russians already left Sakha, but native sakhas have negotiated a peace, and any Russians who don’t leave suffer a spontaneous genocide committed by local peoples.)]

I never thought I’d see Ivan again, but there we were, sitting side by side. We dared not show any obvious joy at reconnecting, so we sat stony-faced as they drove us across the wet and chilly summer night. 
We had been cellmates together for months before being split up, so many years ago, and in that time, we talked so quietly through the night. We talked about everything. But mostly, we talked about home. 
I felt I knew his home in the Urals as well as my own. I looked forward to talking with him again at our first moment alone. The years had been so quietly cruel and lonely. 

#

“There is good news, and there is bad news.”
“The good news, American, is that you will be released.”
Ivan smiled at me, but there was a sadness in his eyes. 
“The bad news, Russian…”
And he shot Ivan dead right there.
“Come on,” he said, with a boyish grin. “You’re going home.”

Hatchlings, Part I: Flotsam
Chapter 00: Darknesses
 
  	It was a new kind of darkness--a shock, for I had come to know darknesses so well. After all these dozens of fives and twos, I'd come to recognize in an instant whether I was waking to the common darknesses of a midnight beating, or the darkness of a burlap sack, or the comfortable darkness of my felted blanket on the packed dirt floor. Those were common darknesses. There were uncommon darknesses too. The forced march in the Siberian night, the smothering darkness of a wet pillow, or the throbbing darkness of a cracked skull. But this new darkness, this was different altogether. This was a queasy, lolling and dripping wet, pierced by thumb-thick shafts of light, sprawled against cardboard box corners kind of darkness.
With newness, fear. With fear, questions. Should I alert my captors that something felt wrong? Or should I shut up and endure? Endurance was so often the only choice, but when changes happened as they occasionally would, it was hard to tell if the Yakuts wanted me to speak up or shut up.
I touched my head--it was bleeding. Had I already received a beating and would be beaten again for forgetting the previous beating? I touched my feet. They were not bound, and that is what made me speak up.
“Hello,” I said in Yakut, “Hello, my feet are not bound. Will you bind my feet?”
No answer.
“Hello, sirs. I need to be bound. Do you wish to bind my feet, and hands?”
No answer.
Wrongness compounded wrongness, and I knew I had to do something. So I curled into a ball among the boxes and let myself be rocked by the lolling dark space.
            #
After what seemed like hours, I determined that the I was floating. Slowly, the rocking sensation had resolved into a clear back and forth, and it wasn’t just the throb of my head. The room, the boxes, everything was somehow buoying on gentle waves. There was a light lapping of water, and behind that sound, there was the sound of shallow breathing. I knew that particular breathing. My blood seemed to slow in my veins, afraid to move for fear of waking the Koryak. I tried to quiet my mind, but it would not quiet.
I must get the attention of one of the Yakuts, and I must do it without waking the Koryak. If they saw my untethered feet, there might be an argument and perhaps the Korkyak would storm off. Or perhaps he would take his anger out on me. But the presence of a Manchary or Ayhal would temper him. Moving carefully away from the Koryak’s subtle breathing, I felt for a ladder, a staircase, a rope. But there was nothing. Just boxes. I looked up at the shafts of light. When my eyes managed to focus, I could tell they were coming from small holes in the ceiling not far above.
I stumbled over the boxes and didn’t get far before encountering a ribbed composite wall. The ribs were sloped the wrong way for climbing, but I managed to pull myself up by the fingernails of my left hand, and almost immediately hit my head on the ceiling, knocking myself back into the boxes. I froze, bracing myself for a beating, but there was none. I stayed still until I could hear the Koryak’s breathing again under the pounding of my heart. He never slept so soundly. Was this a trick? Was he letting me get myself into trouble so that when he pounced, he would indulge his cruelty just that much more? Was I digging my own grave with every move?
I was alone with the Koryak. I had to reach the Yakuts.
In spite of my fear, I tried again. This time, more slowly, I pulled myself up, ignoring the bleeding of my cracked and callused palms as I struggled to hold on to the little flaws and formation-marks on the composite wall. I reached a hand up and felt the low ceiling. There was a rail running along its seam with the wall. Leveraging myself was a triumph of will against my scarred and malnourished body, but I could just bring my eyes to one of the glimmering holes. It was no bigger than my pinkie finger, and when my eye got used to the brightness, I could make out nothing through it except cloud-brushed skyscape. I felt the edge of the hole with my finger. It was a bullet hole.
I had a flash of memory. Shots had been fired. The clickety-clack-whump of bullets ricocheting off composite and burying themselves in cardboard. I remembered somebody muttering in a language I couldn't understand--was it the smoky purr of Cantonese? I remembered a young Chinese man knocking me with the but of a microwave rifle.
Rain dripped into my eye through the bullet hole, dripped down my face. My body was beginning to shake from exertion. Besides the few occasions when the Yakuts made me carry their packs in the early days, and of course the frequent beatings and other tortures, this was by far the most strenuous activity I had undertaken for years. It felt terrible--it felt wonderful. My muscles aching with exertion, my ankles open to the air instead of knocking together between ropes. For the first time, I felt... Well, it must be joy. Joy tinged with fear, of course, as all emotions were so tinged--but still astonishing, unfamiliar yet recognizable, joy.
The rainwater seeped into my mouth through my cracked lips, and into my flesh through the cracks in my lips. It stung terribly, and I tasted it. It was briny. Not rainwater, then--sea water. And I remembered.
I had been on a sea train, and I was supposed to be released.
Released!
I brought my mouth up to the bullet hole.
“Hello!” I rasped, lips chafing against the sharp edges of the composite. “Sirs, hello!”
My legs were quaking fiercely and one of my feet slipped. In the second before my other foot slipped, I reset my hold on the rail, and when my legs went, I swung. No no no, I thought. Don’t let me fall and die. My recurrent death wish had been evaporated by shafts of brilliant light. I knew the boxes were not far below me, but the darkness below my dangling feet felt like infinity--another new kind of darkness. I grasped for my life and kicked my legs in the open air. Tears streamed down my cheeks. No!
My hands slipped, flailing in air. But I didn't fall into the infinite void. I was back in the boxes, heart racing, penetrated through with fear, blind and in pain. But my mind was alive--through the pain and confusion, I focussed on one thing: Release.
I sifted my memories. The last thing I remembered was being let out of a cargo container--that must be what I was in now--and allowed to walk around on the deck of a ship; a sea train. The marine air had been salty and rank, and I found that in my captivity, I had be come an agoraphobe, the vast emptiness of waves in all directions. But the open daylight had been a blessing, and the warm breeze had been an impossible delight after so long in chilly Sakha.
My guards had been a Yakut and three Russians, and they had seemed to be having skipping railgun shots off the translucent sunlit waves, as others tried to vaporize the rails in flight with maser blasts, which usually ended in geysers of steam erupting from the hummocky wavescape. The size of the sea train had been astonishing, terrifying. The barge we were on had stretched perhaps a quarter mile, and I could count at least three more attached barges in front and two behind, all of them stacked high with cargo containers. When I had first been captured, I only knew sea trains to be two or three barges long, and not nearly so large. Much must have changed. I reveled in the memory of a hint of a taste of freedom.
Absorbed, I lay fetal among the boxes.
Movement.
I scrambled, moving in a direction I thought was away from the sound, but as soon as I did, I was on top of the Koryak. His sweaty shirt stunk, but his muscles were slack. He slouched against one of the composite walls, collapsed among the crushed cardboard. I jumped back, and hit another wall, felt along it, tripping on boxes and clawing at the composite all the way. In short succession, I turned three corners and found myself back where the Koryak rested. This container was small, with boxes piled deep enough that I couldn’t tell if there was a floor at all.
Fighting queasy terror, I ran my hands gingerly over the Koryak’s pockets, looking for a gun, a knife, something. The promise of freedom had whet my appetite for survival. I had forgotten that I could want anything at all. The Koryak's pockets were empty. He wore a tool belt, but the only thing on it was a tape measure. I took it, feeling for sharp edges, and briefly flicking a built-in laser level. The sudden flash of red startled me. There was a belt clip, and in desperation, I climbed the wall again with the tape measure clipped to my threadbare jeans. I propped myself in place again, arching my back and pulling until my muscles strained, then swinging the tape measure hard against the bullet hole. Nothing happened, but an echo that seemed to tumble around the container as the we bobbed. I scratched at the hole with the corner of the tape measure, and managed to get the belt clip to catch the rough edge. I worked at it until my arms and legs trembled, and still I persisted. At last, I managed to get the clip all the way through the hole, but I couldn’t yank it hard enough to tear the composite.
So, I put all my weight on it, quickly grabbing with both hands and letting my legs swing. I fell almost immediately and I could feel my skin tear and crack and leak warm blood even before I landed with a crunch on the boxes.
I looked up and saw that I had torn a hand-sized flap into the composite. I gritted my teeth in the closest thing to a smile I could manage, then felt around until I found the tape measure again. When I stopped to bend the belt clip back to a useable position, I heard that the Koryak’s breathing had changed. Shallower, quieter.
More in control.
With a sudden rush of adrenaline, I flicked the laser level towards where I thought his eyes would be.
He chuckled and lunged.
I covered my face and swung the tape measure hard at his temple, but he was on top of me and I couldn’t land a solid blow. His knees were in my ribs, and he shouted in the Koryak language. I bit his arm as hard as I could, not stopping when I tasted blood. He groaned and pulled back, giving me just enough distance and just enough time to slash his face with the ragged metal belt clip. His breathing was labored, but he didn't scream. I did it again, feeling the rough cutting of his flesh with satisfaction, then again, and he was backing away. He hit a wall and slipped and I used the opportunity to fall on him, jamming both my knees into his groin and taking a bash at his face. I kept bashing him with the heavy tape measure over and over again, and with each tear of flesh, each crunch of bone, I remembered the burns, the cuts, the fingernails, and I hit him harder each time until I was bashing nothing but greasy tissue into soaked cardboard.
I shuddered. I had seen such acts often enough, but it was the first time I killed another human being.
My fingers tightened on hair and skin and blood and I stood, pulling the gore away from its source, then I flung it down, wiped my hands on my pants, and climbed the wall for the last time. I didn’t care if I had to swim home. Damn the Koryak. I was free.
At the top of the composite wall, I stuck my arm through the flap I had ripped, feeling cold saltwater bite my wounds. I pulled hard on the flap, and it widened so I could get another hand through, then another arm. I felt a rail on the outside surface and grabbed it with both hands, pulling my body through the hole, letting the composite slice into the skin of my belly, thigh, shins. It could have gutted me for all I cared. The sun was blinding, but I was out and crouching atop the container and I felt around as I tried to strain my eyes open. There container's shiny outer surface was wet and slippery, and I slid a short distance, day-blind, and tripped over another rail.
I tumbled and splashed, and found myself underwater. Now, despite the saltwater sting and the burn of the sun, I willed my eyes open and reached for the shadow of the container, grabbing the rail and clinging tight. I pulled myself above water, folded my arms around the rail, and held on for life.
When my eyes adjusted, I surveyed my predicament. There was nothing but the container's red enamel and open sea all around. The great bulk swayed with the lapping waves. I lifted myself on top of it again.
It had been upended, floating vertically, and I crouched on its narrow end, looking around at open sea and open sky.
Fourteen years as a hostage, and this is how it ends.
I was alone--afloat in the open ocean.
But I was free.
#
It’s hard to say how long I was sitting on that container. Everything of my former life seemed entombed in that cherry red sepulcher, the great gash in its composite skin matching the gashes in my own, a nastily-torn umbilical. I roused from my half-sleep half-daze as the sun set over the craggy waves. It was so beautiful I cried.
I stared at the spot where the sun had set for an apparent infinity, watching stars rain almost imperceptibly over the horizon. The sea became choppier as I stared into the night. Rain began to rain down, washing the layers of filth from my skin. I had had no shower in…Months? Years?
Ayhal had told me they would allow me to wash before I reached home. I grimaced. So they had.
As I rocked in the rain, I let my mind wander. Memories were returning in earnest now.
The sea train was meant to be my ride back home. Manchary and Ayhal said they were trading me for prisoners held in Moscow. My life was worth sixteen prisoners, they’d said, both triumphant and disdainful. I had dared to chuckle. My rapport with the Yakuts had long since mellowed, and when they were around, I had dared to show joviality because I could. Not like earlier on, when the conflict was still fresh and the youthful 'freedom fighters' blazed zealous.
At one point early on, they had decided they were going to entertain themselves with exotic torture techniques.
They laughed and whispered horrors to me as they shopped online for Thai bamboo. They bought the seeds, and taunted me for weeks about how they were going to weight me down and let the fast-growing stalks grow through my torso. An excruciating death, it haunted the dreams I had in what little sleep I got back then. In the end, the seeds had refused to grow in the soggy Siberian soil. I had giggled silently at night for weeks after that in my cell. They only caught me giggling once, and that was worth all the nails on my right hand to them--and to me. You take humor where you can.
I used to talk to them when I could, mostly to practice my Yakut or Russian, but companionship is hard to come by in wartime, and my chats had the effect of easing my captivity sometimes. I suppose that’s why their directorate or overlords, or whomever they answered to had hired the Koryak eventually.
There was no befriending the Koryak. We didn’t have a common tongue, and he had no interest in talking, apparently, to anybody.
The Manchary had told me he’d been a reindeer herder until the reindeer could no longer weather the warming steppes. Strange to know so little about someone you killed. But I supposed that was usually the way of things.
The seaborne rain was calming to a tiny pitter, and I let myself sleep.
#
I awoke feeling like I was falling into a swamp, grasping at a vine that kept slipping out of my hand. When I realized the vine was the rail and the swamp was several inches of water running in thick rivulets into the gash in the container, I panicked. I slipped on the polished surface, and tried to get to the higher end of the rail, but it was no use. My body had been blocking the flow, and when I moved, seawater poured as a torrent into the container's maw.
In moments, the high end had reached the water line, and my ankles and butt were in water. The hole was completely submerged now, and I could see bursts of air shooting out as the huge container sunk beneath me. I held on until I was up to my waist in water, then tried to float, but just at that point, the whole thing descended so fast out from under me that I was pulled after it in the undertow. This was the end, I thought, feeling the weight of the water compress me. The dim glow of dawn receded above me, lost in bubbles and turbulence, and I tried to gasp but only took in water. I was going to drown.
As the surface darkened and I felt the pressure close in on me, something punched me from below, hitting my body with the force of a brick wall, all corners and sharp edges. The impact was so forceful that it made a shockwave of sound that felt as though it would pierce my eardrums. Orangey sunlight appeared suddenly before my eyes, and I was forced again above the surface of the water. I flailed, coughing and gasping for air, and I tried to paddle, but my arms just hit cardboard. All around me, an oil slick of shoeboxes, buoying me. I floated on them, spread eagle, coughing the water from my lungs.
I was woosey, and I must have lapsed into and out of consciousness. I know day and night passed at least twice, but I could not be sure of how long it had been. I felt dizzy, cracked lips and muttering like a drunkard in the moments of consciousness, but mostly I slept.
#
The growling of my stomach woke me.
The boxes had turned to a slimy foamy mush. My body ached with hunger and thirst. There was nothing but shoes and cardboard pulp. I tried to eat some of that. But it was pasty and fibrous and coated my tongue. I had to rinse with the little seawater I could squeeze out of a handful of the mushy box residue, and the effort left my mouth dry and sore.
 
 
 
Chapter 00: Salvage
When the old man found me, he prodded me. And in my stupor, I asked if he was real. He put his hand on my shoulder, and I flinched, but all he did was to hoist me onto the little dinghy he towed behind his jet-ski. I looked around with my cracked eyelids and gummy vision, and saw that he had perched me atop a slimy pile of Nikes. I chuckled, and was out again.
#
A dribbling of earthy-green flavor roused me. It took me a moment to realize it was water, but by that time, my body had already instinctively lapped it up. The old man was using some sort of plant roots to dip and dribble into my mouth from a large algae-laced jar.
He offered the root wad to me, saying “De-saleen,” and I dipped it myself, hesitantly at first, then greedily, sating an infinite thirst over and again until I was glutted on the delicious green water.
He offered some sort of seafood jerky, covered in a white powder, and I took it, touching it to the tip of my tongue. The powder was acrid, acid, metallic and wrong-salty. Evidently noticing my trepidation, he handed me a little condiment packet.
“Tekas bah-bah-koo,” he said, “Mmm-mmm.”
He gave a rotten-toothed chuckle, and I almost smiled. I squeezed the barbecue sauce on the salted squid and ate as much as I could stand. My stomach was full of water, but still churning with hunger. It had been so long since I had eaten.
The food tasted wrong, but I knew I had to eat something.
I lay on smooth wooden planks, and I felt nauseous, as if the whole world were swaying. I looked around; there were improvised shanties in all directions. Men, women, and children peered from most of the doors. These were homes, studded with bubble wrap and Styrofoam and industrial garbage. The sky was clear blue over their rooves, no sign of larger development. The houses were connected by wooden plank bridges and lashed together with replastic ropes.
I had seen such curiosity towards westerners before—we were a rare breed, and getting rarer—but otherwise, it was like no rural village I had ever visited. There wasn’t a single patch of open space; the houses tumbled over one another like a densely packed cityscape. But this was not urban shantytown. It was deathly quiet save for the occasional squawk of a seagull.
As I ate, I stared at the old man. At first, I had thought him a Yakut wearing some kind of traditional cap. But the patterns were wrong…fine threadwork in delicate filigree, tracing elaborate geometries around what looked like an Islamic crescent-and-star. Moreover, his face was wrong; brown-dark instead of ruddy, sunbeaten-leathery instead of cold-bitten leathery.
When he spoke, there was no longer a question.
            “Ya mirá yo con Noe. Noe, Noe,” he said, tapping his chest with bunched fingers.
He repeated the whole exercise several times before I recognized he was speaking some form of Spanish. I hadn’t spoken the language in decades, but I had grown up in the Bronx, and the words came to my lips without hesitation.
“Mi nombre es Lucius. ¿Quién eres tú?” Who are you?
“Ya mirá yo con Noe, soy Banguingui.”
“¿Que?”
“Baa--ngui-ngui,” he said it as if speaking to a child. “Esta gitano del mar. Ya mir--ah…mi nombre es Noa.”
Gitano del mar, I thought. Sea gypsy.
My stomach began to contract of its own accord. The sea gypsy—Noe—grabbed me under the arms and dragged me to the edge of the nearest walkway. He was ancient and stringy, but strong. His muscles felt like two granite bannisters under mine. As he laid me out on my belly, I wretched fish-tinged algae water over the edge into sloshing seafoam a few feet below. Noe braced himself expertly against a taut rope, stretching a leg through the gap and flicking his foot in water to rinse off the vomit.
He left me alone and waved off the others, and I lay there for long enough to notice late afternoon fade into early evening. I heaved a lot at first, then less and less frequently until I felt wrung-out, empty but for a knot of muscle that was my stomach, almost as hard as Noe’s arms.
When the slow rocking of the whole village slowly became predictable, and when I was finally certain it wasn’t just me, and when I was sure I could, I stood. I seemed to myself to be an emaciated cadaver, weeks beyond death. Yet I stood, doddered.
On one side, the village rose towards a boxy, spire-like structure. On the other, walkways extended between shacks and shanties and, strangely, amid all the roughshod architecture, there stood boats of all sizes and styles, embedded in the village, built into the houses--houses built on, around, and through the boats. The whole structure creaked and lurched subtly. Some of the boats were new, but most were weather-streaked, bubbling rusty or sunbaked, crumbly fiberglass mesh. Sea birds squawked about the masts and corrugated fiberglass roofs.
Noe appeared from one of the boats, a formerly-luxury yacht whose bow jutted through a large structure that could've been straight out of the American Old West.
The cobbled-together island seemed built to surround a crescent of open water with small round objects floating in it. I couldn’t tell what they were from here, but they seemed to have plants growing in them. On the broad boardwalk that bordered the tiny artificial bay, there was a small but sturdy-looking brass ship’s bell mounted atop a shiny and equally sturdy brass post. A young girl, barely more than a toddler, climbed the post with two hands behind it and her feet almost flat against the sparkling metal. She rang the bell louder that I would have expected possible for such a small bell, and such a small girl.
He took me by the elbow, and walked me into the yacht. The smell of campfire tinged with burning plastics hit my nose.
A gathering was taking place around a central fire. Smoke pooled in the high ceiling area, and from the deep chocolaty surfaces, I assumed this must have been used as a meeting place for a very long time. Noe introduced me to a young man in a perfectly clean white daishiki, tallish and sharply chiseled, obviously muscular despite the loose clothing, but with the same features and weather-battered look as Noe. He wore a similar Moslem hat as well.
“You will come tomorrow,” said the man.
“Yes,” I said. I had been well-trained to answer such non-questions.
“Yes?” said the man, clearly surprised. “We had feared you would not want to join us. We are going to scavenge, and it is not safe here. But it is decided then.” He said with a smile. “Come,” he said.
The pair, the old one, and the young, led me into a former ball room, where the fire blazed on bare metal, ringed-in by heavy anchor chain, links big enough to put your foot through.
The sea gypsies lined the walls, kneeling in rows four-deep all the way around, six-deep in some places. A young boy held the ubiquitous hat out to me, and I bowed for him to put it on me. It was tight, but it made me feel slightly less awkward.
[Auth. note: Research Banguigui customs, esp interactions with western visitors.]
[Auth. note: See Technical Note 1 on Banguingui language.]
The chiseled young man took me to the top of the tower, supporting me much of the way as I protested on behalf of my atrophied legs. But he was persistent, and when we arrived in a spire peak, I saw why. Over a low rail, I could see the whole town sprawling around the base of the tower. We were afloat in the ocean on an artificial island cobbled together of flotsam.
After the ceremonies, I joined Noe in his family's cabin built atop a catamaran. Through the walls, I heard the rattle of chains clacking across metal. At first, there were only one or two, then a few more, and soon, the individual clatters became one gurgling noise. Noe and his family were wrapping and placing seemingly random household items—a brass vase, a box of tea—into a large metal box. When a woman, presumably Noe's wife, saw me, she protested, saying something in the strange Spanish patois. It was clear she didn't want me there, and I bowed my head to step back outside onto the boardwalk, where the clatter was a waterfall roar.
Seeing the family struck a chord in me. I never did have a family of my own, not a real one. But I remember being part of several. Behavior problems sent me from home to home throughout my teen years, and I never did participate in anything like helping my parents pack a trunk of valuables. There was something about that. I don’t know. It got to me.
Disgusted with myself, I wiped the tearwelling from my eyelids.
“Siento, siento,” said Noe with his hands in the air before him. He shut the rickety honeycomb plastic door behind him.
Noe put a hand on my shoulder, and though it was gentle, I flinched. Noe grimaced at my timidity and almost shook his head. Then, his demeanor shifted abruptly. 
“Durme por Jiho la noche,” he said, laughingly.
#
Jiho turned out to be a tiny kayak suspended above the entrance to the town hall, and accessible only after Noe set up a one-post ladder with a few rungs missing. “Honor grande,” Noe had said, but I suspected the reason they had me sleep with Jiho was because she was visible to virtually every shack and hut in what passed for a central square.
The shiny red kayak was a cocoon, swaying at about twice the rate of the village itself, the crimson glow of the Hall’s stadium lighting filtered through Jiho’s hull cast a shadow across my eyes in the shape of the craft’s name. When the light shut out with a klang! I was alone, floating in the swaying darkness, swaddled in my clothes alone as I had slept every night for the last fourteen years. I got to sleep well enough, but I awoke before dawn with dreams of jelly sea creatures storming the village, prying me bodily from a red locker that must’ve been dream-Jiho.
Noe found me staring down the dock, and put his hand on my shoulder in a gesture that was becoming almost comfortable. I had stopped flinching at any rate.
He pointed to the end of the dock and I saw, like the specks of distant birds in flight, a fleet of what must've been jet skis."Vamos!" he shouted with a krinklefaced chuckle. He shooed me down the dock. "Vamos, vamos, vamos!" he repeated all the while as I boarded the bobbing jet ski. I cranked the accelerator fast, too fast. It tossed me in the water and I flailed and choked and saw a splash next to me that I mistook for an explosion. By the time I realized it was Noe, jumped in to save me, I was in tears with laughter. Noe shook his wet hair, splashing me like a freshly bathed dog. It was my first laugh since I lost my fingernails, and the first genuine, untempered joy since I don't remember.
#
We worked til dusk, skiing from patch to patch, picking pieces from submerged masses of flotsam. When we were about to head back--or, at least, I'd hoped we were--a sound, a shout from the lead skier. A wave of the arm propagated from person to person, back through the dispersed villagers, and like a pack of wild dogs, they fell into one double line.
In the distance, I saw a peak, like a little mountain on the ocean. As the line of jet skis forded the wide ocean into orange pools of sunset, the dark shape grew. It was an island.
No, as we drew closer, I saw it was no island at all. It was a floating yacht, upended so the bow jutted from the water vertically. Around it like seaweed surrounding floating driftwood, was a clot of…of…things. All sorts of things, but mostly plastic zip ties. I had to laugh. It was a bitter laugh. I had spent so many years shackled about the wrists and ankles with such ties. Noe's wife gave me a quick quixotic glance and I stopped.
Following the suit of the villagers, I slowed my jet ski as we plowed through the floating zip tie jungle, and slowly scooted up onto the edge of a more substantial clot made primarily of milk jugs, hardened over in calcarious concretions. The jugs cracked under the hulls of the jet skis, but soon our vehicles were moored as solidly as if we were beached in lime sand.
I looked around, and was reassured that a couple of the villagers were circling out at sea, keeping watch, and carefully, I dismounted the jet ski and tip-toed onto an island of mineralized plastic. The yacht's hull jutted from the concretion of trash, and I used it for balance. As the villagers rounded this artificial peak, they picked among the milk jugs, finding a piece of glass here, a rusted key chain there. One time, a child yelped with joy at pulling a thick silver chain from between the milk jug boulders. As we crossed around the bottom of the boat, a great squawking arose. We had disturbed a bird colony.
The villagers waved their arms, as if imitating the birds, and the birds mocked back, adding to the taunt biting beaks. But the villagers persisted, methodically gathering eggs from guano-covered nests. The eggs were passed back one pair of hands to another, in a train, that included me, and concluded in a pile near the beached jet skis. I passed the eggs along. They came quickly as I could pass. In the brief moment between passes, I tried to fetch a turtle scute that was wedged between the calcified milk jugs, only to find that I was holding up the line. It was the first scornful look I had earned since my time in the village, but it made me smile because I realized I had received not a single rebuke--save for the one from Noe's wife, but that may have been something else--since I had been thrown off the sea train.
At last, my desire got hold of me and I bent down to pick the scute out during a momentary pause in the egg train. It was jammed tight, and I had to stick almost my whole hand, more than halfway up the palm to get a pair of fingers forked awkwardly around its sharp end, like clumsy pincers. I wiggled it back and forth and back and forth for what seemed like a thousand wiggles before it cut me and I yanked back my hand with a yelp. In that moment, I looked up and saw that though I’d blocked the narrow ledge the egg train wound over, the villagers had not been slowed at all by my selfishness. The one in front of me pitched it over my head, higher now that I stood, and the one behind me, a young girl caught it in both her arms before swinging it around to the next villager and snapping round to catch another.
Without taking the time to further consider this odd behavior, I tucked the scute into the fold of my waistband. Immensely satisfied with my efforts today, and warmed in my heart by my fortuitous discovery of the gorgeous, waxy scute, I boarded my jet ski with the villagers and followed them back to the floating village in the sea.
Noe pulled up beside me and shouted, asking after what I found. I slowed to idle and pulled out the scute. It shone waxy yellow-green in the brilliant sunlight.
“My grandmother had a necklace like that. I always wanted one.”
“What happened to the one your grandmother had?”
“She lost it, diving for sea snails.”
#
After three weeks living with the village, eating their food, working their work, it occurred to me that I hadn’t stopped counting days.
For the first fifty-five days of my captivity, I had been plagued by the Yakut guards’ efforts to disrupt me by erasing the marks I made on the walls, or by making new ones. I used pebbles for another twelve days before they caught on, and for three more, I tried to deliberately soil the corner of my pillow once for each sunrise.
The guards’ removal of that lasted for my remaining years until I was loaded in a coffin-sized box and onto the sea train. That coffin-sized box with a pillow was a brief, dark heaven.
On the seventieth day of my captivity, I had spent several hours investigating a clot of crabgrass that grew out from under a small cobble that sat in the ray of sunlight that shone in from the hole in the roof through which my cell was constantly dampened by the cold Siberian rain. I found that if I lifted the corner of the cobble, I could tease the grass roots into a pattern, two parallel roots for each day. Five and two, five and two…one parallel set for each workday and two sets for each weekend. Five and two, five and two. Every week, I twisted up the roots and tucked them into the corner, laying them end to end so it looked like a tree root was slowly sneaking its tendrils along the wall. That lasted me eight hears,  seven weeks, and three days before I realized I didn’t need the grassroots anymore to count the fives and twos. 
I’m not sure why I never stopped counting weekends.
#
The village’s shanties reached in an arc around a central harbor, whose inner edge was lined with low-lying planking. In the middle of it was a tall post topped by a bell that looked straight out of a 20th century cruise liner, all shiny brass, engraved around the rim. Floating in the harbor were what looked like inflatable kiddie pools, half-flooded and bristling with—maybe rice shoots? There must have been a hundred of them, and tending from a tiny catamaran was a young girl and boy that could have been siblings.
#
We were doing a special trip to a place called Rondik to collect mussels.
The skis fanned out, periodically slowing and floating in a wide arc while the leader conferred with elders over current maps. It was almost a days’ trip, but at dusk, we arrived at an atoll surrounding a shallow, sandy lagoon.
Most of the islands of the atoll were barely sandbars, but the largest of them had enough trees to construct a few shelters and moor the jet skis. The rainy season was over, but we were greeted by a late storm and had to build our shelters in the the wind. The mild pelting slowed as the sun set, and the shelters were just enough of a windbreak to construct a fire. We enjoyed a meal of rice wrapped and steamed in plastic tarp, and I was asked to wash the plastic wrap before tucking it back in one of the elders’ packs.
I had not been told to pack for an overnight trip, so I slept in my wet clothes and woke to sounds of activity in the predawn glow.
The villagers were stripping down to their trunks and distributing spears and knives of various makes. Some of them wore nose clips reminiscent of clothes pins.
I stripped down myself, and gestured for a knife.
One of the eldest patted me on the small of my back and asked if I knew how to use it.
“Show me,” I said.
He looked disconcerted and kicked around in the sand until he found a couple of loose shells, which he held together tight, like it was a living mussel.
“Like this,” he said, then he pantomimed prying it up by its base and throwing it in a bag. He then took the shell out of the imaginary bag, pried it open, and cut around the inside, flipping the imaginary meat into one pile and the two shells into another.
#
It turned out I wasn’t good at diving.
Nor was I good at shucking mussels.
“You don’t have breath,” Noe told me, with a sardonic smile.
I tried shucking mussels on the shore, until the others completed them all and went back out to dive for more, leaving me alone on the shelly beach.
#
I wandered the tiny island, circling it. There was little to see here, but the waves. A little ways offshore, there was what looked to be a tree, jutting out of the water. I swam a little ways away from it, but soon found myself wading to a nearby sandbar before laying down on the beach for a nap.
I was awoken sometime later by a great whooping from the expedition lead group. When I got to the beachfront, there seemed little to merit the jubilation.
Just a little ways from where I’d been swimming, the men and young muscled women picked among a flotilla of orange construction fencing and pailsful of yellow rubber duckies. Not an unusual find by any means, nor a particularly rich one. But on closer approach, it was clear their attentions were focused on something still in the water. For a second I thought it was a tree floating on something like one of the kiddie pool rice paddies back in the village. A man stood on a little platform, his hands dancing over a structure that, as he unbent it, resolved itself into some kind of antenna array, all squared corners and anemic crosses.
“What is it?” asked Noe when he got in earshot of the leader.
He grinned and waved us closer.
“Look,” he said, pointing to the base of the antenna.
There was a little black dome just poking above the surface, and on the dome there was a platform and a small, round hatch, battened by six screw-handled batons. The guy straightening out the antenna called for help, and I dove in after a couple of the young boys, taking one of the handles in both hands.
We strained, each of us in our own time, and then in unison, and with several attempts, the lime-encrusted mechanisms cracked into rotation, torquing the squat metal batons out of the closed position. The hatch breathed a belch of chlorine and mildew, and lifted out of its setting.
Having launched myself into the waves, and struggling for leverage to twist the handle I’d chosen, I’d found my footing on something below the lapping water. It felt like the hull of a vessel, and now that was looking for it, I saw ripples surrounding a barely-surfacing fin edge about fifty yards distant. This was a submarine we were opening. And a cheer rose up, first from the kids helping to open it, then from the crowd at large, as we lifted the hatch full open, squealing on paint-locked hinges.
“I saw that earlier,” I spurted. “Before I went to sleep.”
Noe put a hand on my mouth to shush me.
I looked up at him, but Noe was looking up at the leader, and they all followed the leader’s gaze back to me.
He was stony-faced, but beckoned me to take the first step down inside.
The boys on the other handles grinned, and I pulled myself out of the water, putting a leg down the hatch, looking for footing on the now wetted rungs descending into the darkness.
As excited as I was to lead the group, I felt there was nothing I did to deserve it. Here I was napping on the beach. If I’d just swum out a little farther, maybe I could have been the one to find the submarine. This was pretty far from the area they’d focused their dives on. Likely as not, they came across the thing trying to figure out where I’d wandered off to.
The leader selected three of the teen boys to accompany me, and handed down a few flashlights as they descended after me.
I heard one of them whisper as they followed, “Para elegí de un líder…”
Selecting a leader?
It smelled like an old graveyard, so it worried me what the kids might see down here, and sure enough, we came on our first skeleton just as soon as we opened up the next bulkhead. The kids kicked at it and laughed, then rushed out ahead.
“Stay close!” I shouted. But they ran up ahead and I was left to exploring on my own.
#
When we emerged from the first recon, the leader looked me up and down and snorted.
Later, Noe told me what I’d done wrong. I had laid a claim. And it was a big one. I had upstaged the leader in a dramatic way. It didn’t matter that I protested to Noe. I had already claimed first sight of the submarine and had cemented that claim by successfully leading the recon down into it.
This was effectively my submarine. Not mine in the sense of personally owning it. But mine in the sense that all of its contents which would be shared by the village was, in essence, a personal gift to the villagers from me.
#
We mounted return trips to the submarine atoll early every morning. Each day, we stripped it of parts and took turns hand-cranking the makeshift ballast outlet until, two weeks later, the sub’s bay was cleared.
Though I wasn’t much good at stripping parts or wires, I was eventually able to avail myself in the only way I knew how. The Chinese submarine crew had apparently included a large contingent of Brazilians, which meant it had likely been sunk before the Sino-Brazillian hostilities. The upshot of this was that many of the controls had labels in Chinese and Portuguese. I even found a Chinese-Portuguese dictionary in one of the cabins, which let me make rough translations of just about all the controls and documentation.
I suppose as native speakers of a Spanish deprived language, the Banguigui would have been much better than me at puzzling out spoken Portuguese. However, none of them were literate in anything but Arabic.
So, there I was, almost certainly the only Chinese-to-Portuguese-to-Banguingui translator in the world.
The leader did his best to avoid me, and when I tried to meet his gaze, he looked through me.
Despite the chill I got from the leader, the villagers warmed up to me dramatically in those days. I ended up getting to know a lot more of the them by name and personality, roving from room to room as people called my name.
“Lus! Lus! What does this mean?”
More often than not, my clunky translations proceeded from puzzlement through laugh-out-loud error to effective-enough. I’d label each button and switch, and in the process, helped teach many villagers how to read and write their own language. The young women picked it up faster than anyone else, but as soon as they did, the young men followed me around asking how to write shall we say—intimate—letters.
#
The leader had ordered the stripper crews not to touch he electronics of the bay, and when he flipped a great guillotine switch, I saw why.
The dim glow our flashlights was droned out by the blue-green lights of the bullet subs. They were all on the same charging circuit. As the elder electronics expert, Mrs. Noe had joined us today, and she and the leader wasted no time in tracing the extent of the charging system, marking it along the way with a rainbow of brittle old zip ties.
This is what would have to come out intact.
For the remainder of the dry season, the stripping crews and the bullet sub extraction crews worked side by side, finishing their respective jobs in the sub’s bay, deftly repositioning to avoid getting in each other’s way.
#
It had been several months, and I was growing accustomed to their…hospitality wasn’t the right word, since I worked hard for my keep, if not every bit as hard as someone like Noe, then at least as hard as some of the young women. Whatever the word for my relationship with Noe and his people, they were kind to me, and I to them.
We always worked hard, but those months in the submarine atoll were the hardest. Long days every single day.
 
I missed weekends in Montreal.
 
The first group home I lived in, I used to do kitchen duty. Seven or eight years old, I’d set the auto-clean on the ovens while I strained the crumbs out of there frites fryer and wiped down the waffle iron and griddles, under the watchful eye of Miss Alejandro, who everybody called Lehan. I was up before dawn every morning except Saturday and Sunday. 
 
When I got a little older, I worked at an Outlet World in Mississauga. Twelve hour days sorting bins of other peoples’ discarded clothing. I hear there used to be laws about that kind of thing. But for us there were no breaks. I just ate a big meal of scrambled eggs and toast in the morning and a pile of plain fries when I got off. It didn’t pay well, but at least it was safe compared to the jobs my friends took at tire recycling plants and fisheries on the lake. Saved up just enough money for the island ferry, and the occasional trip to Montreal on the weekends. That, at least, was one break from it all. They could work you to the bone all week long, but the last of the unions fought like hell to keep the weekends. Even as the rest of the labor laws wend down the drain, we managed to enshrine the weekend as a god-given right. Five on, two off.
 
#
A few fives and twos into my stay with the Sea Gypsies, and I found myself invited to stay with Noe and family. They’d rigged up a little rowboat to hang underneath a trap door in the brushed aluminum compartment that served as their kitchen, a few feet above the frothy ocean. They gave me a few minutes of time alone in the kitchen every evening to wash up, but in the mornings I awoke to the smell of “Mrs. Noe” and her daughter, whose name I never learned, cooking sticky rice.
Mrs. Noe’s missing front teeth left her looking like she was permanently sucking at her upper lip, but when she smiled, it was hard not to be drawn in by her genuineness.
I had gained weight among the sea gypsies. Before my captivity, I'd been a bear of a man. The girls on campus had taken turns riding piggy back at the bar every weekend, but the Yakuts had poured atrophy upon me. 
Now that I could eat and work, my muscles were returning. I was still lean, but my biceps and cavs were starting to bulge. It seemed comical. And still, I couldn't laugh. 
When I noticed this, I stared at my limbs for hours after a Saturday lunch.  The family left me be, lighting a candle for me when night fell. I sat up late in the greasy light, massaging my muscles as I tensed and released. At dawn, I let out a light sigh and blew out the candle, counting it an accomplishment. 
In those few moments of dim dawn light, I washed my face in the aluminum sink that looked for all the world like a repurposed commode from the old 947 jetbus that first flew me to Moscow, complete with the blue ring stain. I poked around the kitchen before bedding down, still amused by the novelty of a room that wasn’t bone-bare, picking up a loofa, feeling its strange fibrous squeeze between my fingers, picking up a knife makeshifted from a piece of aluminum siding. I always put the items back where they came from, in exactly the position for fear, I think, that Manchary would comment on my curiosity, and the chuckling would bring the Koryak.
    There was only one thing I didn’t put back where it belonged.
One evening, in the dark of the oceanic night, I caught a glimpse of reflected moonlight shining from the cracks in the cupboard under the sink. I swung the cupboard door open to find a box full of people shakers, most half-filled with the residue of salt melted by seawater and dried again into a crust. The tops of the shakers were beautiful, slightly yellowed silver with lightning traces of deep blue iridescent patina.
     The one thing I missed in my captivity was salt. All I ate for years was boiled potatoes or kasha, with no seasoning of any kind. I used to scrape my own sweat, on lucky days when it got warm enough for me to sweat, and smear it on the back of my bed plank, to scrape it off in tiny amounts as salty wood flavor. Now, here at least two dozen salt shakers with salt still inside—apparently unused--for the villagers seemed to prefer their packets of soya. I had to have one, so I took one.
I took to helping the family in the kitchen, exchanging trivialities, smiles, and the occasional cigarette. Noe made faces at the daughter, drawing laughs from her, and the family in turn. I tried to join but my Banguingui did little good when it came to inside jokes with long family backstory.
#
A storm was coming, and I cradled my precious salt shaker that night, swaddled in rags in my rowboat swinging from the underside of the kitchen. Those nights were nice, cool, and calm. And the time passed slowly, pleasantly. 
There was a light rapping as I climbed into my rowboat, curling around my salt shaker, as a piece of flotsam buoying against the floats that bore the village. I couldn't get to sleep and I decided to climb down and take care of it. 
The waves were fierce, but the enormous artificial island barely rocked. From the rigging underneath, the village was posts and dangles, hulls and floats. Most of the upside area was shanties, and below this there were great telephone-pole props leading down to enormous flotation devices like bobbers of a variety of constructions—some were repurposed aluminum tankers, others were  great plastic pontoons cobbled together of myriad drinking bottles like the egg sacs of giant spiders of childhood nightmares. 
The source of the rapping was a brittled plastic baby carriage tangled in a net around one of the moorings. The net was tough, and to get it undone, I would have to follow it down and over to where the lower docks protruded onto the open sea. 
I leaned to watch the villagers, all elbow deep in storm prep. I’d asked to help, but had been ignored, or given duties so trivial I felt more in the way than not. The rocking of the village had been ringing the Centerbell since early that afternoon. Between the posts mounting the planked areas to floats, I saw Noe shimmying up with duct tape in one hand and bubble wrap in the other. A feat in this downpour. He compounded the feat by somehow getting the tape to stick as he wrapped up the bell like a package for delivery.
The bell was silenced.
In the distance, I could see the swells of the open ocean, gray black like charcoal, backlit by—the moon? No. I could see the moon’s reflection on the waves’ frontsides. I stared, only feeling a sense of how large the swells were by their slowness of motion. Glacial, tectonic. When one had subsided, another had risen behind, also backlit, obscuring the source of whatever light was out there.
Dangling by an arm and propped up by a foot, I swung back and forth on the mooring post, giving in to sluggish rocking. And stared. Into the moon-mirrors, into the black coal, staring. Staring until the incident angle of the light source in the distance reflected off a wave-wall to port. And in that moment, I saw what looked like a ghost city painted on the water.
I squeezed my eyes closed and open a few times, and lost it in the distance. Would there be islands nearby? I’d assumed we were in deep ocean. I hadn’t yet begun to wonder where we actually were in the ocean. Crossing directly from the Russia far east to the American west coast would have taken the sea train across the north Pacific. But in my time with the Banguingui, there seemed to be no change in the seasons. It was mostly hot and dry. I assumed the dry season would give way soon enough to the wet season. If so, then we’re much farther south than where I was tossed overboard.
I spoke with Mrs. Noe that evening, not mentioning the phantom island.
“Are there islands around here?” I asked.
“Rondik,” she said.
“That’s a day away.”
“Now. But we will move closer.”
“Does the village travel far?”
She nodded, drawing a big oval in the air with the knife she’d been using to shuck barnacles. She made the oval again, this time narrating the circuit: “Caledonia, Marshall, Australia…”
I didn’t know how to ask whether the village can be steered. “Always the same path?”
She considered. “Sometimes different.”
“So, no islands near here?”
“Long ago,” she said. When I gave her a questioning look, she went on: “Water takes land,” she said, holding the knife horizontally and lifting it as if raising the sea.
Her Banguingui was more idiomatic than Noe’s, clipped here and there, with lots of words that sounded…maybe Filipino.
“Where do the people go?”
“They move. Like us, some. Some go to other land. Kiribati. ”
 
#
The next night, I decided again upon climbing down onto the dock and into the open. Again, I marveled at the relative calm under the village was a universe away from the chaos outside. Though the storm had us well in its grasp, all below was swaying ropes and bobbing floats and light ocean spray. Waves tumbled over the end of the docks, and an awful wind hurled raindrops of all sizes,  pelting my face like gravel. Saltwater spray twisted my vision, but through the noise like a million pounds of frying fishes, I clearly heard the screech of grinding metal wailing from across the waves. 
I forced myself to look into the blast. There were lights shining from inside a giant skeletal dome. It was bucking the waves, so I couldn't estimate it's heading. 
I scrambled up the inundated rope ladder to the courtyard. Deserted. After all that activity this afternoon, people had hunkered down with their families to ride it out. 
I screamed at the top of my lungs, but the incessant rain drowned me out. The Centerbell stood mummied in bubble wrap, but I climbed the pole and tore it open like a present. The lights from the dome dazzled over the village, and my heart pounded. The rim’s lettering was visible from here: [Auth. note: The bell’s rim is a quote from Rime of the Ancient Mariner or maybe the Tempest.]
I rang the bell as hard as I could, the wrappings flying off in the wind. The rain had let up, and the bell's ring cut through the wind's swish and whip. 
I clung to the pole, unable to take my eyes off the looming dome. It reared in the waves, its barnacle-crusted leading hull slowly rising high above the docks below. I heard a yelling from the village and a series of sharp whistles, just before the Goliath dome smashed down on the docks, pitched so I could see over its jutting ribs to the dwellings in concentric rings. 
And then the quake. Wrenching metal squealed so loud I thought my eardrums would rip. My fingers squeezed tight on the cold metal but I knew I would slip several long moments before my fingers gave out. My mind refused to accept it even as I tumbled, thinking "My foot's still on, if I only could hook it around," then I slammed into the planking. 
I sprung up, dazed, and looked at all the villagers looking at me...no, they were looking past me. I staggered round. The courtyard floor curled, splintery, upwards like a crashing wave, but torn down the middle. Through the great gash, the village in the dome was visible, and its doors were opening.
Dazed villagers from the dome stared up at our dazed villagers. And the wind howled. My head spun. 
There was stillness of all but the downpour as everybody absorbed the situation. What looked like a lightning bug squiggled through the rain and landed on my neck. I swooned, stood straight, saw the blur of people running screaming into battle through blurred vision, then collapsed. 
 
 
 
Chapter 00: Silverware
I came to again, to a chaos of noise and motion. I was hunched against a wall. Noe was standing beside me, trying to light the wick on what looked like a pipe bomb. The rain had settled down a bit, but the wind stifled his flame. There was a low rail and I couldn’t see what was happening beyond.
I tried to stand, collapsed.
“Get him out of here,” said one of the other villagers.
I got to my knees and looked over the rail. The villagers still clashed on the splintered planks below, all sinew and blood and electric discharges flitting about. Within the battlefield throng, three piles of dead bodies had formed; one whose faces were strange to me, and two whose twisted faces were all too familiar.
The invaders were armed with objects reminiscent of exterminators’ equipment—thin tubing that sprayed electrical discharges in ways that looked impossible to aim. Each one led to a white plastic football-like tank dangling from the fighter’s belt. As absurd as it seemed, they were able to take down five men at a time, making strangled sounds as they collapsed, twitching.
A few of our men were swinging machetes or boat paddles, but the majority of men, women, and children just piled on the invaders, ripping with bare hands at weapons, hair, and faces. We seemed to outnumber them.
I stood and put an arm over Noe’s shoulder.
“Take him away,” insisted the villager again.
“No, let me help,” I managed.
I was able to shield Noe’s lighter from the wind long enough for him to light the wick. He threw it, almost before I was sure it was lit, and pulled me down to the duckboards with him, covering his ears.
Before I had time to cover my own, the explosion rocked below us. We looked over the railing and saw the carnage we’d wrought. The body of an invading fighter was twisted, wrapped around the centerbell pole, a gaping hole in the deck where he had just presumably stood. We were destroying the village as fast as we were destroying the enemy.
#
As the battle battle died down, and we spent much of the day chasing off those left behind by the retreating islanders. They fought and were killed, or they swam off in the night, no doubt to their deaths.
#
I was just settling into sleep-dark when I heard the deck-muffled sound of strangled screams, male and female, and clingety-clang of someone frantically rifling through silverware. I came above decks to see Noe and his daughter unconscious or dead on the kitchen floor.
The invader turned, a mask of anger carved deep into his face.
He had a heavy, squarish face that didn't quite fit its skin. Like a brick in a thick wool sock. His age was indeterminate. His skin was sun-blackened and his hands were rough and calloused white. They grasped a handful of forks and spoons.
Then he was a blur, throwing the utensils down and I was slammed into sleep-dark when something concussed violent on my cheek. A bloody salt shaker tumbled across the floor. I inhaled blood and coughed and felt the cool wind probing a gaping hole torn in the skin of my cheek. He lunged but I kicked him in the kidney and he rolled into fetal position while I fell to my knees, woozy.
I pressed the ball of my hand into where my cheekbone should be, felt crunchy grinding and winced. Dazed, I pulled myself onto my feet. The intruder had dumped the kitchen drawer all over the floor, and the contents of Noe's family treasure box on top of that. Sorted piles stood alonside the mess. He’d apparently been triaging the valuables.
I tried to kick him in the ribs, but he turned and kicked back, knocking one foot out from under me, still clutching a silver chain. Fucking pirate. He rolled into my other foot, and I tumbled, splattering my bloody cheek on the deck boards. He was on me, holding my neck. He was strong but skinny. His skin seemed thin.
I pictured my hand grabbing his neck, and it happened. At once, my thumb and fingers clamped down, wringing a fistful of tendons. He screamed but I held tight. 
He writhed. 
I pulled back my other arm, bringing my fist down on his delicate cheekbone. It shattered and he cried, wrapping hi skinny arms around his head. I realized he was just a boy. 
#
I backed up, righted myself using the kitchen counter. I took the aluminum knife from the back of the sink and waited for him to lunge at me. When he didn't, I nudged his shoulder with my foot. He rolled onto his side, crying. He pulled his jacket around his chest, and two silver salt shakers tumbled out, tocking across the floor.
And then I stabbed the fucker under the jaw.
As he bled out, I started to giggle. At first, just a little thing. Then a chuckle. Then I burst out with a laugh that sounded like a cry of pain, doubled over like I was vomiting laughter all over the floor. I couldn't help it, and I couldn't stop it. His blood crept towards me and I laughed myself breathless.
When Noe came to, I was on my hands and knees, howling hysterically in a pool of blood. I could barely see him through the tears in my eyes, but I heard him.
"Jesuchristo,” he mouthed before rushing to tend to his daughter.
In my adrenaline, I hadn’t realized she too was bleeding.
“I will take Nisha to the elders. Leave this,” he said, gesturing to the mess of goods and the lifeless body on the kitchen floor. “We will take care of it in the morning.”
 
#
I spent the darkest hours of the night awake in my swinging compartment, watching to see if the blood would drip through the cracks in the floor’s aluminum welds. But it never did.
I finally fell asleep to the sounds of Noe cleaning up the body and putting away the silverware at dawn.
#
Waking up late in the afternoon, I was surprised to see that the only evidence of my struggle last night was a shinier-than-usual kitchen floor.
I was beginning to understand he villagers' lingo  pretty well. So, when I tried to lend a hand, lugging a bucket of sand to the largest of the remaining fires, I was taken aback when the young man said, "No, sir," and reached to take it from me. 
"Is there something else I can..." 
"No, sir. Please go," he repeated. He shook his head vigorously, holding his hat and bowing.
I had just gotten well enough yesterday to get out of my rowboat, and when I'd exchanged good mornings briefly with Noe, I'd gotten the sense he was giving me the cold shoulder. I assumed I deserved it for what I'd done to the pirate kid. 
I didn't want too look for him now, but I felt even more out of place and confused than usual. The other villagers seemed happy enough to see me, if not eager to interact.
When I did track him down, he was chill as I'd expected. He was jubilantly tearing out melted wiring from a seized pirate jet ski but his face dropped when i approached. I asked if there was somewhere I could be useful. 
"I don't think they would let you," he said. 
"Why not?"
"You should ask them," he said.
"Well, can I help you?" 
He sighed. "Go to the Hall. Maybe they'll let you help there."
The villagers were exuberant with work. The pirate attack had left a lot to be repaired, but the surprise victory charged the atmosphere. Kids whistled while they worked and old women clapped at each other's efforts. 
Outside the village hall, I caught up with a young man, one of the leader's attendants I recognized because he'd been beating the biggest drum at the gathering. 
He was swinging an old pair of headphones like a bola, singing a modulated tune in pseudo-Arabic. When I approached, he shrieked, "Ha-ha!" and struck a pose right out of an old Robin Hood movie. "There he is," he declared, letting the headphones fall at my feet. 
I was dumbstruck. This was the most any villager besides Noe had ever said to me unprompted. And now, when Noe didn't seem to want anything to do with me.
"What do you want?" he asked.
"What do you mean?"
"Whatever you want," he said, "I was told to provide for you when you came. If you came."
"Why?"
The boy laughed, shaking his head. "Don't you know you're a hero man?"
"So," he said, "what do you want?"
I laughed.
“I don’t know,” I said. “What should I want?”
#
I spent the day on a small deck jutting off of the village’s spire, watching television as the sun set on the horizon. I felt bad not helping in the work, but the boy had insisted.
[Auth. note: What does he see on television?]
I had dozed off by the time the young leader showed up, and his hand on my shoulder jolted me awake.
He looked at me gravely, and I offered to pitch in with the building of that evening’s fire, but he said no.
“Necesitamos hacer cosas para que sepan que apreciamos a nuestros héroes.”
We should do things to let it be known that we appreciate the heroes.
“I’m not a hero.”
He waved this off.
The leader’s attitude towards me seemed to have chilled. He seemed cautious, but resigned. He squinted a bit as he considered me.
Then he pulled up a chair and pointed at the television.
“You know this one?” he asked. “Watch.”
Some kind of melodrama interspersed with explosions and aggressive sex scenes. I wasn’t sure what to think about this. I hadn’t thought about sex in so long. We watched with the sound off, and the leader burst out laughing whenever somebody got hurt.

"You okay?" she said.
"Yeah," I nodded, actually feeling lighter than ever, happy even. Really happy.

[How much background to include here? We should get an impressionistic overview of Sakha and the conflict told through Luce’s interpretive lens.]
 
#
 
[BACKGROUND ON THE CONFLICT IN SAKHA]
 
The conflict in Sakha is driven largely by environmental and technological changes which are reshaping Siberia’s biogeography and demography. Genetically engineered crops, especially those featuring more efficient chlorophyl allows a dramatic increase in agricultural products, and an accompanying population boom. This creates a shift in the power dynamic in which local and regional governments whose newfound economic importance feel they have the muscle to push for greater autonomy. This plays into the ethic tensions that simmered under the surface for centuries, particularly resentment at the Russification which has proceeded apace since the 18th century. While the actual extermination of the colonial era subsided, the waves of Russian colonists from the west and the cultural assimilation of remaining ethnic minorities led to near extinction of many local cultures.
 
Usually referred to as “The Magadan Conflict” after the city in which hostilities first broke out, even though the stakes of the conflict primarily involve the independence of the neighboring Republic of Sakha. The name of the territory is in dispute. Under Russian authority, it’s been referred to variously as the Republic of Sakha, or the Autonomous Republic of Sakha. However, the independence fighters refer to the area as Yakutia. There have long been rumblings for independence from the indigenous Sakha/Yakut people, but it wasn’t until the ethnic Russians, who dominate the area, decided they want independence from Moscow that the conflict really boiled over. These Sakha Russians take on the mantle of Yakut independence in their propaganda and  but generally treat the yakut people as a political soccer ball. Thus the sides, broadly speaking, are: Moscow-loyalist Russians vs Pro-independence Sakha Russians. Ironically, most Yakuts are now against independence. Yakut ethnic extremism was once a rare, very fringe phenomenon, but it was very violent when it did happen. However, in the decade before the Sakha rebellion, the Yakuts were radicalized by neighboring Buryats whose own independence movement flared on and off for years before burning out with no resolution due to sheer exhaustion of forces. Despite promises to the contrary, Yakuts generally fear the Sakha Russians will gain even more power in an independent state. However, some Yakuts, especially young men, do side with the independence movement and form a large chunk of the fighters. Many of them realize they’ve gotten in over their heads. Various other Siberian ethnic minorities have mostly tried to stay neutral, although many individuals may side with one group or another. These include the (thoroughly russified) Itelmen, who tend to side with Moscow, the Evens who mostly side with the Yakuts, and the Koryaks/Chukchis who have largely tried to keep out of the fray.
 
Beyond the main conflict, a variety of other players have been involved, and the independence movement is only the nexus of a continent-wide political and social upheaval.
 
The breakdown of infrastructure in Sakha and surrounding areas has provided an opening for rival powers in the broader region. While the far eastern regions of Siberia remain nominally loyal to Moscow, the conflict zones have effectively cut them off from western Russia. In this power vacuum, Chinese interests have prevailed. The Chinese (PRC) government has maintained official neutrality, but haven’t been entirely covert about encouraging Chinese nationals to take advantage of trade opportunities in the now cut-off areas. They call these policies “emergency trade rules” and assert they are only in place for humanitarian reasons. In practice, however, the policies seem to be an effort to undermine Russian authority and assure Chinese domination in the post-conflict world, no matter how the conflict turns out. The reality on the ground is that the vast majority of “Chinese trade envoys” (ostensibly meaning private merchants encouraged to open up trade into Russian territories) are, in fact, members of organized crime groups whose most obvious activity—“hostess clubs” (brothels)—are largely fronts for moving resources and stolen goods out of Russian territory. Diamonds are the most obvious example, but everything from various mineral ores to jewelry and silverware are being extracted in a virtual torrent of goods. Meanwhile, on the way into the region are arms, drugs, armies of sex workers, enormous gambling establishments, and liquor (most notoriously knock-off tar/kymys (aka airag/ayran/kumis)—fermented mare’s milk favored by Yakuts, but often mass-produced with cow’s milk—laced with formaldehyde, heavy metals, and who knows what else). Along with the crime, an increasingly large transfer of population from China is happening as roadside stands become permanent fixtures, and up-and-coming Triad leaders take over small villages with their newfound economic muscle. In the countryside located well away from the hottest conflict zones, it’s not an uncommon occurrence to see parades of SUVs celebrating a young Chinese mobster’s wedding to an even younger Siberian bride, rolling past brand new McMansions with freshly-filled in ground pools.
 
In contrast to the Chinese interest in solidifying central Asian hegemony, the Japanese government maintains a truly hands-off approach. But not to be outdone, Japanese organized crime has taken a keen interest in the area. Centering on Vladivostok, the Triads have set up a similar array of grey- and black-market activities. Since their interest in eastern Siberia is really just a cover for involvement further west in Sakha, they don’t really care about the Triads so long as they keep their activity restricted to the eastern coastal area. Thus, the Triads have had free reign in a narrow strip extending from Amurskiy Bay to the coast across from Sakhalin island, rarely venturing farther inland than the Republic of Amur. A visitor could be forgiven for believing that the Yakuza now ruled Vladivostok outright. An additional wrinkle has emerged as the conflict drags on. Chinese Triads have ceased to be satisfied with restricting their own activities to the conflict zone proper, and have expanded southwards into Mongolia, along the main contraband thoroughfares, and east towards Vladivostok. Recently, it has become clear that there is a simmering turf war between the Chinese and Japanese gangs.
 
The interests of organized crime generally align with each other, leading to competition. Chinese Triads want some order because they rely upon an effective infrastructure to move massive amounts of contraband, but they thrive under diminished rule of law. Similarly, Japanese Yakuza like the relative order of eastern Kamchatka, but need the Triads to remain occupied in the conflict zones. The long term goal of the Triads—and perhaps the Yakuza—is to have the the Sakha government firmly in their pockets, regardless of which side wins.
 
NGOs have had a limited humanitarian impact because the Chinese government has argued their own efforts at encouraging trade are sufficient. To their credit, they have actually underwritten many of the itinerant merchants who supply most staples and durable goods. Whether this underwriting actually translates into lower prices is a matter of widespread debate. Of course, the PRC’s ulterior motives are fairly apparent. Instead, NGOs have focused on conflict resolution and communication support.
 
Another major player is the Almazy-Rossii Sakha Diamond Company (ARSDC), which has vowed strict neutrality. Along with humanitarian NGOs, Almazy-Rossii is among the few entities who have official dealings with both the Moscow authorities and the Sakha provisional government. ARSDC is trying to consolidate its monopoly, but management conflicts have left the company teetering on the edge of a breakup. They’ve been determined to continue production, and they hire partisans from both sides (since the outbreak of open conflict, their near-poverty wages are considered very good). Their operations are the closest thing to a neutral zone in the region. “ARSDC” is jokingly referred to among the NGO crowd as “Arse-Dick”. The Mirna Diamond Mine is the company’s primary regional home base.
 
The conflict has also seen a great deal of demographic shift as populations have fled the area, while other populations have streamed in. Sakha has become increasingly Yakut in demographics as Moscow-loyal ethnic Russians have fled westward. Ironically, the remaining Sakha Russians have tightened their grip on power in the Republic as the de facto government has made greater and greater wartime concessions.
 
Interestingly, there has been a dramatic increase in Koryak population. This started before the conflict, when warming-driven expanded grazing lands led to a resurgence in traditional pastoralism. More and more urban Koryaks sought to return to the traditional lifestyle, often mortgaging houses and pawning valuables to purchase reindeer and yaranga (yurt) materials. Coinciding with this return to the land, many young couples chose to have children, to complete the traditional lifestyle, and for the first time in living memory, the Koryak population experienced a baby boom. But the traditional ways require traditional wisdom, and few were able to transition successfully into self-sufficiency. Looking for ways to keep solvent, many turned to selling reindeer meat, reindeer kymys (fermented reindeer milk, aka airag, ayran, kumis), and crafts to tourists in urban centers. But a sudden glut in the market, combined with a decrease in tourism due to the conflict in the neighboring territories, led to a wave of bankruptcies. Many Koryaks caught up in what was to become only the first wave of bankruptcies, sold or rented their herds to other up-and-coming wannabe pastoralists. Those who didn’t, sold to banks, who turned around and re-sold. The second wave of bankruptcies hit even harder, and the now swollen ranks of neo-pastoralists faced a debt crisis that ended in widespread poverty, crippling two generations of Koryaks, with no end in sight. Alcoholism, suicide (and sometimes familicide), and crime skyrocketed. Though they had a recently-strengthened tradition of fierce independence, a significant minority of Koryak men were swept into the ranks of the Sakha rebels. Others, sensing more opportunity with the wealthy regime in the west joined Moscow-loyalist fighters, leading to brother-against-brother warfare where both brothers are proxies for outside forces.
 
The Koryak language had been nearing extinction in the 20th century. Most individuals spoke Russian and the one-time linga franca of the region, the closely related Chukchi language, even at home. However, the resurgence in neo-traditional culture led to a generation of Koryak monolinguals. This linguistic insularism further exacerbates the economic and social problems that now plague the Koryak people.
 
PRO-INDEPENDENCE: Most Sakha Russians, some Yakuts. Unofficially, China (PRC) who hope to provide technical assistance to the booming mining industry, as well as a big cut of the profits.
ANTI-INDEPENDENCE: Some Sakha Russians, almost all other Russians, most Yakuts, Sinophobic Chinese expatriates
SEMI-ALIGNED: Chinese Triads, Japanese Yakuza
MOSTLY UNALIGNED: Itelmen, Chukchis, Koryaks, Evens, Evenks, Buryats, ARSDC
 
ARSDC
Russian Name: Алмазы-России Саха Даймонд Компания
Transliteration: Almazy-Rossii Sacha Diamond Kompaniya
Translation: “Diamonds-Russia Sakha Diamond Company”
Acronyms: АРСДК/ARSDK/ARSDC
Pejoratives: Arse-Dick, Assdick, The Cartel, The Company
