[TECHNICAL NOTE 2: Challenges of Jovian exploration, physiological effects of magnetic fields and radiation]
 
[Technical note: NASA has speculated on the feasibility of mining the atmospheres of the outer planets, particularly for helium-3, an isotope of helium that is rare on Earth and could have a very high value per unit mass as thermonuclear fuel.[77] Factories stationed in orbit could mine the gas and deliver it to visiting craft.[79] However, the Jovian system in general poses particular disadvantages for colonization because of the severe radiation conditions prevailing in Jupiter's magnetosphere and the planet's particularly deep gravitational well. Jupiter would deliver about 36 Sv (3600 rem) per day to unshielded colonists at Io and about 5.4 Sv (540 rems) per day to unshielded colonists at Europa,[80] which is a decisive aspect due to the fact that already an exposure to about 0.75 Sv over a period of a few days is enough to cause radiation poisoning, and about 5 Sv over a few days is fatal.[80][81] In 2003, NASA conducted a conceptual study called "Human Outer Planets Exploration" (HOPE) regarding the future human exploration of the outer solar system. The possibility was mooted of building a surface base on Callisto, because of the low radiation levels at the moon's distance from Jupiter and its geological stability. Callisto is the only one of Jupiter's Galilean satellites for which human exploration is feasible. The levels of ionizing radiation on Io, Europa and Ganymede are inimical to human life, and adequate protective measures have yet to be devised.[80] Equipment sometimes requires isolation from external magnetic fields. For static or slowly varying magnetic fields (below about 100 kHz) the Faraday shielding described above is ineffective. In these cases shields made of high magnetic permeability metal alloys can be used, such as sheets of Permalloy and Mu-Metal,[5] or with nanocrystalline grain structure ferromagnetic metal coatings.[6] These materials don't block the magnetic field, as with electric shielding, but rather draw the field into themselves, providing a path for the magnetic field lines around the shielded volume. The best shape for magnetic shields is thus a closed container surrounding the shielded volume. The effectiveness of this type of shielding depends on the material's permeability, which generally drops off at both very low magnetic field strengths and at high field strengths where the material becomes saturated. So to achieve low residual fields, magnetic shields often consist of several enclosures one inside the other, each of which successively reduces the field inside it. Because of the above limitations of passive shielding, an alternative used with static or low-frequency fields is active shielding; using a field created by electromagnets to cancel the ambient field within a volume.[7] Solenoids and Helmholtz coils are types of coils that can be used for this purpose. Additionally, superconducting materials can expel magnetic fields via the Meissner effect. The squareness (saturation remanence divided by saturation magnetization) and coercivity are figures of merit for hard magnets although energy product (saturation magnetization times coercivity) is most commonly quoted. Magnetostriction (cf. electrostriction) is a property of ferromagnetic materials that causes them to change their shape or dimensions during the process of magnetization. The variation of materials' magnetization due to the applied magnetic field changes the magnetostrictive strain until reaching its saturation value, λ. This effect causes energy loss due to frictional heating in susceptible ferromagnetic cores. The effect is also responsible for the low-pitched humming sound that can be heard coming from transformers. Sendust is a magnetic metal powder that was invented by Hakaru Masumoto at Tohoku Imperial University in Sendai, Japan, about 1936 as an alternative to permalloy in inductor applications for telephone networks. Sendust cores have high magnetic permeability (up to 140 000), low loss, low coercivity (5 A/m) good temperature stability and saturation flux density up to 1 T. —Can a sintered sendust core explode due to overly high magnetic fields?— Hiding from Jupiter’s radiation: http://www.astrobio.net/news-exclusive/hiding-from-jupiters-radiation/ Jupiter is the largest planet in the solar system with a radius of over 70 000 km. While 20,000 times the intrinsic strength of Earth's magnetic dynamo is incredible, it is approximately a dipole field. Given that Jupiter's radius is almost 12 times that of Earth, and that dipole field strength drops off with the cube of the radius perpendicular to the dipole axis, one would project a surface field on the order of 7 Gauss at the equator from just the ratios of the equatorial diameters. The only Galilean moon safe enough to do a landing and EVA on would be Callisto. All other missions would have to be robotic, and even then radiation would mess with the computers.]
 
[Technical note: Magnetic explosions—Periodically, a probe coming passing too deeply into Jupiter’s magnetosphere, or encountering magnetic anomalies, may explode due to its magnetic shielding deforming too quickly. Similarly, high currency spikes caused by magnetic induction can cause electronics to explode. “Magnetic reconnection could be the Universe's favorite way to make things explode…Magnetic lines of force cross, cancel, reconnect and—Bang! Magnetic energy is unleashed, with charged-particles flying off near the speed of light.” Jupiter's magnetic field is 20 times stronger than the magnetic field on Earth. Unexpected increases in the magnetic field can come as a result of interactions between the fields of Jupiter, its satellites’ fields, and the Sun’s field, sometimes causing a “foamy” texture to the field.]
 
[Technical note: Biological effects of powerful magnetic fields…the magnetic taste, nausea, vertigo, esp in fields over 4 Tesla. (Jupiter’s magnetosphere isn’t anywhere near this, but perhaps is occasionally focussed to higher levels in regions where it interacts with other magnetic fields.) Electromotive force induced by blood flow in a static magnetic field. Rumors of faster aging, tinnitus, numbness. The Jovian system could feel haunted…more a result of rumor than anything. …a very small magneto- hydrodynamic force operating on the endolynphatic tissues of the inner ear may be the source for the sensations of nausea and vertigo sometimes reported at higher field strengths… the time variation of a magnetic field induces into the patient an elecric field according to Maxwell's third equation… These gradient-induced electric fields, at sufficiently high values, could stimulate nerves and muscles and, at very high levels could generate cardiac stimulation or even ventricular fibrillation… ]
 
[Technical note: Ghost signals: Jovian equatorial magnetic field strength: 428 μT (microTesla). MRI typical values are 1.5 or 3.0 T. Really, only the radio radiation is a significant risk (if you have metal, due to focusing burns)…but that doesn’t stop rumors. However …to place a human in a rapidly changing magnetic field that is powerful enough to induce currents in neurons in the brain. Transcranial magnetic stimulation (TMS) can cause/treat hallucinations. Only if fields are tightly focused, which is rare…generally(?) with fields in the 0.5μT range (ranging from 4.2 gauss (0.42 mT) at the equator to 10–14 gauss (1.0–1.4 mT) ). Focus the field in the visual cortex, for example, and the induced eddys cause the subject to ‘see’ lights that appear as discs and lines. …rapidly changing fields associated with repeated lightning strikes are powerful enough to cause a similar phenomenon in humans within 200 metres.nTo be sure, this is a rare event. The strike has to be of a particular type in which there are multiple return strokes at the same point over a period of a few seconds, a phenomenon that occurs in about 1-5 per cent of strikes…]
 
[Technical note: Liquid magnetic shielding?]
 
[Technical note: Spherical cows…Reilly applied his electrode numerical stimulation models to determine gradient-induced nerve [49] and cardiac stimulation thresholds [50, 51], and predicted electric field amplitudes required for stimulation as a function of waveform (pulse duration, waveshape, and pulse train length). Reilly simulated the patient as an uniform cylinder with radius R = 0.2 m and with the axis parallel to the static magnetic field https://static-content.springer.com/image/art%3A10.1186%2F1475-925X-3-11/MediaObjects/12938_2004_Article_38_Equa_HTML.gif . Thus, the value of the z-gradient induced electric field was calculated by the formula: Reilly compared the values of the electric field obtained by his model with the experimental results reported in the literature and suggested that the best approximation for nerve stimulation threshold was an exponential curve. A better approximation might have been made using an hyperbolic form, which appears to be a better fit for more recent experimental data [52, 53]. Thus, it was estimated that the mean cardiac stimulation threshold was a factor of 2 above that of the most sensitive population percentile (1%), and the mean threshold for cardiac fibrillation was estimated to be another factor of 2.5 above the mean for cardiac stimulation. Figure 3 shows the mean thresholds for peripheral nerve and cardiac stimulation for the most sensitive population percentile. If the ramp duration of the time-variation of the magnetic fields is less than 1000 μs, the margin between nerve and cardiac thresholds is large, but if ramp duration exceeds a few milliseconds, the mean peripheral and the cardiac nerve stimulation thresholds approach each other [54].]
 
[Technical note: Other biological dangers of magnetic fields… …cardiac stimulation in the most sensitive population percentile should require 20 times the energy needed for the peripheral nerve stimulation mean. When a static magnetic field is applied to a biological tissue, and ionic currents are present, a net force; applied to moving ions… The effects of EMF on the stimulation of nerve or muscle cells have recently been studied in humans at field strengths as high as 8 T… At the highest field strengths currently available, the flow-induced current densities are below the threshold levels needed to cause nerve or muscle stimulation effects, and no significant vital sign changes, e.g., ECG recordings, have been reported at these high field strengths. ...if the products of a chemical reaction are more paramagnetical than the reactants, the presence of a magnetic field could shift the reaction equilibrium to increase the concentration of the products. A very common and important chemical reaction in humans is the dissociation of oxyhemoglobin (diamagnetic) into separate molecules of oxygen and hemoglobin (both paramagnetic). In this case, an externally applied magnetic field could lower the energy barrier for the dissociation. Calculations indicate that, even in an applied field of 1 T, the free energy barrier to dissociation is changed by only 1 J/mol [36]. Such a small energy shift has less effect on the reaction equilibrium than does a temperature change of 0.01°C. Another chemical effect of the static magnetic field consists of the modification of the kinetics and recombination of radical pairs reactions. Free radicals are supposed to be involved in harmful reactions in biological systems, thus any effect that might increase their reactivity or concentration could produce or enhance a harmful effect [37]. From this observation, the radical pair mechanism has been proposed as a working hypothesis for possible adverse effects of magnetic fields on biological systems. In fact, according to the most accepted theory [38–40], the magnetic field splits the radical pairs into two energy levels, this increases the amount of radicals pairs that escape the recombination reaction [41], i.e., the concentration of free radicals. Experimental research has shown that weak magnetic fields may reduce the second-order decay rate constant of the reaction [42]. Several studies analyzed the effects of magnetic fields on the recombination reaction of radical pairs in micelles and confirmed that an externally applied magnetic field increases the number of radicals escaping the recombination reaction [37, 39, 43]. However, few studies have been performed on biological tissues or on animals and humans. Claims that low magnetic fields damage health have led to extensive medical, chemical and physical research: though no firm evidence of hazards has emerged [44]. …it was reported that the auditory evoked potentials of a subject exposed to a static 0.35 T magnetic field was phase-shifted [24]; the phase shift slowly (15 minutes) returned to normal after termination of the magnetic exposure. However, further studies did not confirm these findings [25, 26]. Pacini, et al. in 1999 [27] described the effects of the static magnetic field generated by a 0.2 T magnetic resonance tomograph on a normal human neuronal cell culture. They observed that after 15 minutes exposure, cells showed dramatic changes of morphology, developing branched dendrites featuring synaptic buttons. …a motion-induced electric field, and it can produce biological effects by disrupting physiological electrical signals of the human body, such as neuronal conduction and biopotentials. ECGs of monkeys exposed to a strong static magnetic field showed field-induced morphological changes in T-wave shape. (In electrocardiography, the T wave represents the repolarization (or recovery) of the ventricles. The interval from the beginning of the QRS complex to the apex of the T wave is referred to as the absolute refractory period.) …these changes were explained by the presence of the electromotive force (EMF) induced by blood flow in a static magnetic field, which is proportional to the quantity we can run into situations where the energy is concentrated in an unexpected and undesirable way, usually by certain types of metal. Under certain conditions this could result in heating of the metal and a burn in the surrounding tissue. MRI uses a combination of magnetic fields and radio waves. MRI generates a net magnetization vector in the human body…the gradient magnetic field, which produces different resonant frequencies for aligned protons, depending on their spatial positions on the gradient axes; these gradient fields allow for the spatial localization of bi-dimensional MRI slices and hence the reconstruction of three dimensional MRI images…the radio-frequency electromagnetic wave, centered at the proton resonant frequency, which rotates the vector…dielectric permittivity of the material…the conductivity…the current…For biological tissues, both the dielectric permittivity and the conductivity are strongly non-linear functions of frequency…at low frequencies, electromagnetic fields interact at the cellular or multicellular level; as frequency progressively increases, bio-electromagnetic interactions occur with the cellular membrane and intracellular organelle, followed by molecular interaction and, finally, at microwave frequencies the field interacts only with water molecules…Materials that cannot be permanently magnetized are characterized by a physical parameter, the magnetic susceptibility (χ), which describes their behavior when placed in a magnetic field…measured by the magnetic dipole moment per unit volume…Materials may be classified into three large groups according to the values of their susceptibility: diamagnetic materials (-1 < χ < 0), paramagnetic materials (0 < χ < 0.01) and ferromagnetic materials (χ > 0.01). The safety issues associated with exposure to static magnetic fields have been discussed for more than a century: in 1892 Peterson and Kennelly [18] studied the effects of the exposure to the largest magnet then available (approximately 0.15 T). They exposed a dog and a young boy to the whole-body magnetic field, finding no positive results. About 30 years later, in 1921, Drinker and Thompson [19] investigated possible health consequences of exposure to magnetic fields in industrial workers. They performed numerous experiments in vitro, on nerve-muscle cells, and in vivo, on living animals, and they concluded that the static magnetic field had no significance as a health hazard. Since then, several studies have been performed, and a review [20], published in 1962, collected about 400 reports dealing with biological effects of magnetic fields. According to Schenck [17], the portion of this literature dealing with supposed pathological or therapeutic effects of magnetic fields is contradictory and confusing. Moreover, basic information, such as the field strength and its variation over the body, is not provided. Interest in the biological effects of static magnetic fields has increased with the invention of MRI at the beginning of the 80s. In the last twenty years, several studies were carried out in order to understand the potential hazards associated with exposure to a strong static magnetic field. The majority of these studies did not report positive results, thus postulating no adverse effects for human health. In 1981, Budinger [21] summarized the work done previous to that date, concluding that from an analysis of the vast literature on cell cultures, animals, and men, no experimental protocol was found that, when repeated by other investigators, gave reproducible positive results. Twenty years later, Schenck [17] confirmed this and concluded his review stating that, because of the difficulty in establishing a negative conclusion, it should not be concluded that it has been proven that there are no significant biological effects of static magnetic fields. However, the steadily increasing capability to realize ever stronger magnets gives reason to believe that such effects could eventually be established, but probably at field strengths well above those currently utilized in MRI. In a relatively recent report [22], no adverse biological effects were found after sub-chronic (10 weeks) exposure to a very high magnetic field (9.4 T) in adult male and female rats and in their progeny. There was a statistically significant (p < 0.05) finding for sensations of nausea, vertigo, and metallic taste in subjects exposed to 1.5 and 4 T static magnetic fields, but no statistical significance was found for other effects such as headache, hiccups, tinnitus, vomiting, and numbness. A higher incidence of positive reports originated from those subjects exposed to the 4 T field. However there was no evidence that these effects were at all harmful.]
 
[Technical note: The Magnetic Day—Jupiter’s magnetic field rotates most rapidly of all the planets with a period of only 9 h 55 min 29.7 s. Maybe the platform rotates with the magnetic field, but there are electrical plants that cross the field lines intentionally to generate and store electricity that the station can periodically rendezvous with to charge up. What happens if a rendezvous is missed?]
 
[Technical note: How do Jovians survive the high radiation and magnetism?]
 
 
[END TECHNICAL NOTE]
 
[TECHNICAL NOTE 3: Rogue planets, space chemistry]
 
[Technical note: Rogue planets have been ejected from their planetary system. We investigate the possibility that a rogue planet could maintain a liquid ocean under layers of thermally-insulating water ice and frozen atmosphere as a result of geothermal heat flux. We find that a rogue planet of Earth-like composition and age could maintain a subglacial liquid ocean if it were ~3.5 times more massive than Earth. If a rogue planet had about ten times higher water mass fraction or a thick cryo-atmospheric layer, it would need to be only ~0.3 times the mass of Earth to maintain a liquid ocean. Such a planet could be detected from reflected solar radiation and its thermal emission could be characterized in the far-IR if it passed within O(1000) AU of Earth.]
 
[Technical note: The pressure at the bottom of the ice layer is ≈ 9 MPa for each kilometer of ice, scaling with mass as (M/M⊕)1−2v ]
 
[Technical note: The melting point of pure ice is 250–270 K at pressures less than 620 MPa (Choukroun & Grasset 2007), although contaminants to pure ice such as chloride salts or ammonia could suppress the melting point by ≈ 50 − −100 K ]
 
[Technical note: In steady-state, the temperature at the surface of a Steppenwolf planet (Ts), i.e., the top of the ice or frozen gas layer, will be set by a balance between thermal emission and geothermal heat flux, F = σTs4, where σ is the Stefan–Boltzmann constant. Astrophysical radiation backgrounds (Mathis et al. 1983; Dole et al. 2006) are negligible. Any gas present in the atmosphere at planetary ejection or outgassed subsequently by geological processes will tend to freeze into a low-thermal-conductivity blanket that could allow T0 to exceed Ts. A blanket formed by freezing Earth’s current atmosphere would only increase T0 by about 4 K…Isostatic adjustment (Fowler 1990), however, will tend to cause some continents or islands to rise above the layers of water and ice on an Earth-like Steppenwolf planet with an active mantle, allowing volcanoes to continuously emit gasses that can freeze onto the water ice surface. Here, we will consider carbon dioxide because it is likely to be outgassed in significant quantities from an Earth-like planet and it supports a stable layer with relatively high base temperature relative to other common gases. To find the temperature at the carbon-dioxide layer base, we note that the thermal conductivity of car- bon dioxide again scales as T−1 (here, in a more lim- ited regime), but with constant of proportionality A ≈ 100 W m−1 (Sumarokov et al. 2003). We find that the maximum temperature supported is robustly ≈ 220 K for Earth-like Steppenwolf planets and is determined by the weak temperature dependence of the melting curve (Giordano et al. 2006). Setting T0 = 220 K reduces the required steady-state water-ice thickness by an order of magnitude. At Earth mass, the temperature at the bottom of a layer of solid CO2 reaches the melting temperature of CO2 for a layer thickness of ≈ 2 km, or ≈ 3×106 kg m−2. Venus’ atmosphere has a partial pressure of CO2 of ≈90 bar (≈ 106 kg m−2), which is roughly the vapor pressure of carbonate rocks at Venus’ surface tempera- ture, implying that there may be more carbon locked in rock in equilibrium with the atmosphere (Pierrehumbert 2010). The store of carbon in carbonate rocks in Earth’s interior is uncertain, but the continental crust is esti- mated to contain the equivalent of ≈ 7 × 105 kg m−2 CO2 and the mantle may contain 2–4 times this amount (Zhang & Zindler 1993). Therefore it appears reason- able to assume that a Steppenwolf planet could have a sufficient complement of CO2 to significantly elevate T0.  Since the viscosity of ice depends strongly on tem- perature (Barr & Showman 2009), if ice convection were to occur on a Steppenwolf planet, it would occur only in the lower, warmer ice regions and would be capped by a “stagnant” conducting lid (Solomatov 1995). …detection of reflected sunlight in the op- tical wavebands and IR follow-up present the only viable observational choices in the near term.  For a single-visit limiting magnitude r ≈ 24.7 of LSST (Jones et al. 2009) (and comparable r = 24 in the nearer-term Pan-STARRS (Jewitt 2003)), and albedo of 0.5, the limiting distance out to which an object can be detected with reflected sunlight is ≈ 830(r/R⊕)1/2 AU. The baseline requirement to identify a Steppenwolf planet is a detection of thermal emission in the far-IR. Photometric microlensing has also been proposed as a method to detect rogue planets throughout the galaxy (Han et al. 2004). If each stellar system ejects one M⊕ planet, a survey like the Galactic Exoplanet Survey Tele- scope could anticipate ∼20 detections of rogue planets…If a Steppenwolf planet harbors life, it could have origi- nated in a more benign era before ejection from the host star. Alternatively, after ejection, life could originate around hydrothermal vents, which are a proposed loca- tion for the origin of life on Earth (Baross & Hoffman 1985). If life can originate and survive on a Steppenwolf planet, it must be truly ubiquitous in the universe.]
 
[Technical note: Common elements and chemicals in the universe: H, He, O, Ne, N, C, Si, Mg, Fe, S...carbon dioxide, carbon monoxide, water, ammonium, methane… formaldehyde, alcohols, acids, aldehydes, and ketones…polyaromatic hydrocarbons (PAHs), amino acids, nucleobases, silicates,  kerogen-like carbon compounds, ices, CN, aliphatic hydrocarbons, glycoaldehyde (a sugar found in RNA), HCN, HNC, H2CO, glycine? Common elements in the Earth’s crust: silicon dioxide, magnesium oxide, ferrous oxide, aluminum oxide, calcium oxide, sodium oxide, ferric oxide, titanium oxide, chromic oxide, manganese oxide]
 
[END TECHNICAL NOTE]

### TITLE: Crossingtide
(c) 2008 by Nathan McKnight 

Shepherd Quickdart fluttered out of the sediment, stirring up clouds of turbidity that startled the villagers. He smelled the perpetrator in the current from the cool river runoff. He knew his quarry well. He’d been chasing the polyamator as he skipped from estuary to estuary, trying to blend in before he committed his next cross-contamination. 
Quickdart slid along the bottom, quietly swimming upstream. The effort was invigorating. It reminded him of last Opposition's breeding season, when the currents of his home river were especially strong and cool. He missed its smell, missed making love to its waters. But his mind was pulled to the present when caught another whiff of the criminal, shivering with disgust at the thought of the polyamator spawning in *his* river. 
The sediment in this river was thick and murky, typical of the polyamator’s haunts. It made him cough, but he kept quiet as he slithered between the roots of the sand trees. Come breeding time, this estuary would be a throng of spawning families during Opposition, but this close to Passing, it was a ghost town. 
There was a sound to his left, a swish of water, and a startle of motion. Quickdart’s tail snapped, just grazing flesh, drawing blood, then darted in pursuit. The flow of the chase blurred his eyes even as it cleared his gills. The perp dashed under a log, then popped back out at Quickdart’s face and fled over his shoulder. Quickdart turned and pursued him back downstream towards the village, hearts beating in cascade. 
"Choke on sand, Shepherd," shouted the perp, before he disappeared into the crowded market. 

Quickdart swam upside down above the heads of the shoppers, trying to catch the polyamator, or spot him with his eyes. The behavior drew stares from the villagers. He heard the sound of a scuffle at the edge of the market, and darted towards it.

A bone merchant writhed in front of his hole, bleeding from a deep ventral wound. Quickdart rushed up to him and waved a stranger to go find a poulticer. "Which way?" he whispered to the dying merchant. But he was too far gone.

Quickdart swam into the merchant's hole, jangling bone scrapers and needles as he rushed through narrow corridors. There was nobody here, but the hole exited into a deep fauna-encrusted crevasse. A tiny tended garden swayed at the edge of the crevasse, beyond which cruising along the jagged bottom would be impossible. 

Quickdart cursed. Lost him again. 

But just then, he heard the gurgle of somebody struggling to hold their gills shut. 

"Give it up, polyamator," said Quickdart patrolling the edge of the garden, always keeping his body between the merchant's dark hole and the garish fauna. 

"My name is Leantail," he heard a voice say from behind a stand of polypflowers. 

"Whatever your name is, you're out of options. One way or another, it's surrender or fight, so you might as well come out now and we'll get this over with."

"I can still flee," said the criminal, his voice rasping with desperation.

"You've already fled from estuary to estuary, contaminating every river you've touched. Would you appreciate strangers spawning in your homeriver? There's nowhere left to flee."

"You don't get it," said the polyamator, "I'm from the outskirts of the great delta. My little stream is a cesspool from all the riff raff that show up for Opposition. And now, every year, my spawn mingles with millions. I can't even make myself make love to my own water. What am I supposed to do, roll over and die of gametosis?"

"Listen Leantail, I'm from the city too. It's hard, but you have to accept your home for what it is. I did, and so do most of us. And however hard your life is, that's no excuse for spawning in *multiple* streams."

"Well, lots of others die. My estuary is unlivable, my offspring are doomed if I spawn there. Hopping from stream to stream is the only chance I have. I can still flee," he repeated. 

"There's nowhere to go, pervert. Flee into the crevasse if you want. You'll get caught up in the firebranches or sucked up by a hexapus. I'd rather take you into custody, but if you want to kill yourself, that would solve my problem just as easily." 

"I can still go *up*. The worlds are passing tonight, I can cross to the frontier."

"Are you crazy? That's a long swim, even from here. By the time you get to Highpoint, you'll be exhausted. You couldn't possibly make the crossing alive."

"What do you care?"

"Because you're a polyamator and a killer, and I'll be damned if I let you get away, even to the frontier."

The garden was quiet but for the waving of bristleweeds. 

The perpetrator slithered from around the polypflowers. He was a sullen adolescent, his gills lined with dark streaks from mudhuffing. 

Quickdart approached cautiously, feeling sad for the kid. Nothing could end well for this one. He gave Leantail a sympathetic look, but reached out to loop a twine around his forefin. Just when he was within reach, the kid lunged, knocking Quickdart back. His dorsus stung with bristleweed quills and the kid fled into the bright water above. 

Quickdart's Shepherd training kicked in and he swam into the sky in persuit. The village vanished in the depths, and he could hear the astonished gasps of shoppers below. 

If this kid was serious about crossing, he either had some serious grit or he was suicidal. The prospect terrified Quickdart, and he was a trained Shepherd of the Law. When the frontier planet passed overhead and the twin seas kissed, there were only a few minutes to make the crossing, and it was a struggle against a raging current that, when Passing was over, was flung out into space, a monstrous spray of rapidly-freezing seawater. Quickdart had heard stories of whole families ejected into space and raining down again, frozen into gigantic balls of hail. If the sparse reports from those rare souls who claimed to have crossed *back* were to be trusted, you'd find yourself on your own on another planet, in a barren land populated by society's rejects, with nowhere to return to during Opposition. No family stream. No home estuary. Quickdart shivered. 

It was insane, but every year one or two people tried. Most of them were thwarted by the Crossing Guard, and those who evaded them were usually killed in the process of the crossing itself. It was news whenever somebody actually made it across the sea bridge. And whatever happened to them after that, nobody knew.

Leantail thrashed violently, an inefficient swimmer, but he was young. And with his head start and Quickdart's bristle-stinging skin, he was far out ahead. As the hours rolled on, the kid shrunk to the distance, a dark speck soon to disappear. Quickdart whistled the call of the Crossing Guard and swam on.

He thought of his home estuary being polluted or overrun by throngs of strangers. What would he do? Would he keep trying to return home? Would he be able to make love to the water of his own familiar river once it was no longer sweet and cool? It would kill him inside to have to make love to murky water. But what could you do? You have to spawn. Everybody has to spawn. Opposition was half a year away, and the longing for high tide was strong. Quickdart let himself imagine for a moment what it would be like to spawn in a home estuary not his own. The smell of unfamiliar water, the keening of strangers. It made him feel dirty, queasy. He shook off the feeling, ashamed of thinking such sick thoughts. He would rather die of gametosis than commit such a cross-contamination.

He had been swimming for hour upon hour, and the light from the shallows above was dimming. This was the highest he'd ever swam, and he was feeling light headed. His eyes and gills tingled. The Crossing Guard should be close, but he had trouble whistling. A strange whine permeated the water. 

Quickdart strained to see what was happening above. He could see the annual eclipse starting, a massive shadow creeping across the water. 

"Leantail!" he shouted, "Give it up. This is crazy." 

He wheezed with the effort. The water up here was fizzy, and breathing it so hard was pure stabbing pain. Above, he saw saw a group of shadows swimming into the eclipse. As they converged, he started to catch up, and he could make out what was happening. They were going to apprehend the polyamator. Relief flooded over Quickdart.

In spite of the pain, the fizzy water seemed to be energizing, and he swam faster. He wanted to be a Scene of this one. He'd been persuing the polyamator too long to see him captured by volunteers. 

The frontier planet was a behemoth looming above. As its crescent became clearer, the blue of foreign seawater glinted brilliantly. The guards surrounded the kid before Quickdart could get there, and he felt a mixture of relief and disappointment. He slowed down to approach and let them do their job. The kid hovered motionless. He looked tired and Quickdart could see blood seeping from his gills. The guards were talking to him in reassuring tones.

Damnit, thought Quickdart. This wasn't the right approach. He swam up to the group, and one of the guards turned, surprised. 

"Grab him," shouted Quickdart.

But the kid used the distraction to stab two of the crossing guards between the eyes.

Quickdart surged forward and tackled the boy, narrowly dodging the sharp spine of his tail. They wrangled for a moment, but Leantail slipped out of his grip.

"You can't go much higher," shouted one of the guards as the kid swam for the sky with Quickdart in persuit. 

The kid trailed cloud of blood and adrenaline. 

The whine in the water was getting louder. The surface of the sea was in sight, and it roiled. He had never seen it so close before, but he'd always heard it described as mirrory and placid. This was anything but.

The water seemed to curve upwards, into a hill reaching towards the frontier planet above. Leantail swam up into the bulge as it stretched upward, thinner and thinner, like a tunnel. Quickdart could see great masses of water separating themselves from the sea surface and flying up towards the other planet. Currents swirled, carrying foamy white streamers and small fishes. At the surface, he could see floating tree branches and debris circulating. The kid was cornered again. 

"Don't come near me," he said. "I'll kill you."

Quickdart tried to grab him and Leantail tried to stab back, but both of them flailed as a surge of current swept them into the tunnel of water above. A roar rose and the water went white. 

Quickdart's eyes and gills felt like they were going to be pulled out of his head, and he swam upward, feeling frantically around for the kid. 

An alien smell washed over him, and the whiteness passed. Suddenly, he found himself in the center of a vast bridge of perfectly clear, ice cold water. To one side, a planet. To the other side, a planet. Leantail curled, fetal in the middle of it, surrounded by stars. Quickdart's hearts beat in cascade, and he took the kid under his fin. 

From the position of the sun, Quickdart was able to determine which world was his own, and he began to swim towards it. 

The swimming was hard in this corridor, and his fins felt like they would freeze. He swam towards the whitewater below, tugging the unconscious kid along. 

Though he was battered and exhausted, he couldn't help but feel struck through by the beauty of swimming in the sky. He could see the entire world spread below. The blue of ocean, the long peninsula of the continent stretching over the horizon. It was terrifying.

Suddenly, the ocean bridge constricted, and Quickdart instinctually pushed the kid out ahead, towards home. The effort pushed his own body back, towards the frontier. He had felt weightless, now he felt like he was falling, and he swam forwards behind Leantail's limp body. 

In an instant, the wall of ocean surface appeared in front of him. He could see Leantail on the other side of it, in a wobbling tail of water extending to the home world. And he knew he was not going home. Forgetting what that meant, Quickdart turned and swam for his life. The frontier world below seemed to be getting bigger, the water seemed to be getting colder. He glanced back at his home, but was startled by the snap of sea surface closing off again, separating the tongue of water into another floating globe behind him, then another, and another. He imagined himself getting caught in one of those, to freeze into a giant ice ball and spend the rest of eternity in the ice ring around the sun.

He swam as hard as he could into the unknown below, foreign seawater engulfing him. The foamy expanse of whitewater loomed, and then with a concussion that deafened him and knocked his gills shut against his sides, a wall of sea surface cut him off ahead. He reeled. The waterscape around him had coalesced into a wobbling sphere. He swam in a little circle, seeing there was no way out. After a lifetime in open water, it was deeply confusing. The seconds ticked forwards over what seemed like ages. The frontier planet below was growing rapidly, and home was shrinking rapidly. The surface of the mini-sea he floated in seemed to be calming as it fell, and white bubbles of ice seemed to be multiplying along the leading edge. 

Quickdart felt his muscles weakening, and his Shepherd training kicked in. He swam in wide circles, skimming the edge of the sphere, hoping to keep himself, and the water around him from freezing. He couldn't feel his body at all, but he could tell he was moving. Dizzy, nearly blacking out, he swam, swam, swam. Through foggy vision, he saw the frontier planet expand out, flattening.

Then a crushing pressure wave knocked him unconscious. 

The smell of the home estuary filled his gills. He floated, serene. 

Then he blinked awake, bobbing in the sea. Claps of hail thundered in the distance. A surge of flotsam struck him, and he felt bones crunching in his side. He panicked, swimming down. The hailclaps sounded louder and louder. A ball of ice plunged past his face, sinking deep, and he kept swimming. Another plunged past, trailing bubbles. He swam on. His body was numb but his gills felt hot. Huge hail boulders plunged on all sides, and others rushed up from below, flying back towards the surface. The sounds of cataclysm wracked his body, the cracking of ice like the cracking of enormous bones. He wasn't sure if it was the hail stones smashing against each other, or his own body being beaten pulpy. 

Darkness closed. Warm water felt like burning, but it was good. He swam towards the seabottom, the echoes of Passing quieting behind him. He swam as deep as he could until he found a warm current, then relaxed, hoping the current would carry him somewhere. Some foreign estuary he could make his home. 


### George and Maurice
by Nathan McKnight

George and Maurice shared everything; a womb, a teat, a cradle, a heart, a kidney, a bladder, half a liver—
they shared alienation and hatred, existential confoundment, exactly one short-lived relationship, and the
pain of rejection. And against it all, they shared humor, they shared strength.
George awoke early one morning, with the new rays of an old sun shining across his face in all but a long
shadow cast across his eyes by Maurice's bony old nose, and in a shared nursing home bed, George turned
to Maurice.
“Mo,” he said, “Mo. Let's go, Mo. I have to use the bathroom. I figured you could help me.” His chuckle
at the tiny joke woke up his brother, as he knew it would. Maurice's eyes opened. He looked thoughtful,
breathing quietly through his nose. Then, Maurice spoke, saying “Who are you?”

### TITLE: The Appetite (unfinished short)

(c) 1999 by Nathan McKnight


Celeste didn't notice the sharpie smell until the surgical assistant—if you could give the lanky, dreadlocked twentysomething such a proper sounding title—marked the second X on her thigh, but when the plasticky smell hit her nose, she thought the headrush would do the job of anesthesiology as well as whatever they were planning to give her anyway. 

“Is that where the tube's going in?” she said. Dr. D just raised his eyebrows and pursed his lips to the side. 

“Give me a hand with this, then get that dog out of here,” he said to the assistant, and before Celeste could object, he jabbed a syringe deep into her tricep. 

“Rachel...” she tried to say as her mouth went all mushy.

The surgical assistant pinched the muscle where Dr. D had poked, and kneaded it painfully. Celeste dazily followed the contours of the ceiling's water stains with her eyes as she sunk deeper and deeper into the Lay-Z-Boy that served as an operating table. She yawned. She yawned hard. And then she sunk so deep that the chair enveloped her, so deep that she was five feet below the floor, so deep that she, Celeste, was all the world but for the blurry face of the surgical assistant that stared at her through a hand sized hole until even that faded away.




* * *


“Eat a shit hot dog!” Balaune shouted, ignoring the middle finger the guy flicked over his shoulder on the way out the door. “Next!” Shouted Belaune. “I said next! Step on up, hon, you want your license or not?”

The teenager between the worn velvet stanchions stammered. 

“Don't mind Belaune,” said Celeste as she waved the boy up to the counter, “just try not to stare at her boobs.” 

“It's not that he stared, of course he gonna stare. It's the way that he stared. Belaune's melons demand respect, baby.” She turned to the boy, “Now, what can I do for you honey?”

Celeste leaned back in her chair, sipping her White Castle coffee. It was sweet and velvety. Tuesday mornings were about the only time the DMV wasn't busy, and Davy was at a managers' meeting, so Celeste and Balaune had the place to themselves for once. 

After stamping the boy's temps, Belaune asked him “Now, what do you think of Celeste there, she's real pretty ain't she?” 

“She's got a pretty face,” he said, then “I mean, I'm not saying the rest of her's not...” 

“Nevermind, hon. Sometimes it's better to keep your mouth shut. Kay, bye bye now. Scooch!” The boy sauntered off and Belaune turned to Celeste, “Aight, look. It's like this—some dudes they like sticks—beanpoles, like those teenage hoes always hanging out in front of Subway's over there. Other guys are into curvy ladies like Belaune here. I ain't gonna lie to you, girl, a lotta guys like the curves. But there are some guys out there they're all about the bbw's like you. See, you just gotta find your audience, babe. Like Melton, he's all about you.”

“Belaune! You always tell me I should divorce Melton.”

“Well, yeah. But... look, point is, there's someone for everyone. And not even someone, there's lots of someones for everyone. It's just about findin them. Anyhow, you're losin weight ain't you?”

“Oh my god, you finally noticed!” Celeste couldn't keep herself from grinning. She'd been walking back and forth behind the counter all morning just to get Belaune to say something. 

“I noticed the minute you walked in the door, hon. But what'm I gonna say—damn that ass look tight?” 

“Yea-ah! That's exactly what I was waiting for you to say.”

“Well...” said Belaune, looking down at her pink-and-green-fern-painted fingernails, “...day-amn that ass look tight.” They both laughed, then Belaune said, “So... you ain't been workin out—I know that much. Wha'chu been doin, poppin ephedrine or somethin?”

“You know me too well. I got a really good diet from my sister.”

“Serious? How you like it?”

“It's good.”

“Come on, what's it all about?”

Celeste scratched her thigh where the surgical tape tugged a couple stray hairs. “Um, it's like a cross between Weight Watcher's and... you know that Dolly Parton diet that was going around years ago?”

“Dolly Parton diet? Haha, no. I never heard of that one. Wrong culture, I guess. Anyway, does it look like I need a Dolly Parton diet?”

“I know, right?” said Celeste. “It's like you have to eat this special soup but you can eat as much as you want of it, and for a whole week all you can eat is bananas.”

“Bananas?” 

Belaune looked skeptical, so Celeste went on, “Yeah, and you have to keep track of every calorie. It's hard but it sure pays off, right?” 

“How many calories in that coffee?” asked Belaune.

“Oh, um... a hundred and fifty I think?”
“You are so full of shit, Celeste. You ain't on no diet and you know it. You think you can lie to me and I ain't know it?” She shook her head. “Mmm'mmm. So, come on. Why you keepin secrets from Belaune? I thought we was tight.”

“I can't tell you,” said Celeste, “I don't want it to get around, you know? Because then everyone else will be doing it and the price will go up.”

“Oh. My. God. You got a liposuction, didn't you?” 

“How did you know?” 

“Cause I'm exactly the same way, honey! You think I look like this naturally? Uh'uh girl! I got my stomach stapled when I was twenty-three. Before that, I was a plumper. Make you look like Miss Ethiopia 1985. Anyhow, how much you pay? You make your man pay for it?” 

“Ahem.” A voice from behind startled Celeste. “You know, if you sit on an airplane toilet and flush it, it'll suck your guts right out. Loose weight quick that way. Or, you could always get some exercise by re-numbering the dottle database like I asked you to do last week.”

“Oh, shut up Davey,” they said together, then turned to their separate monitors to re-number the DOTL database.


* * *




“I got us some KFC on the way home,” said Melton, “mmm-mmm-greasebuckets.”

Melton arranged the chicken crusties on the old pizza tray on the coffee table in front of the TV, poured out a couple glasses of coke.

Afterward to The Appetite by ChatGPT, November 13, 2024
McKnight’s The Appetite examines the toxic relationship between body image, self-worth, and societal expectations. Celeste’s story unfolds as both a personal struggle and a commentary on how society pressures individuals into viewing their bodies as flawed. Her interactions, especially with coworkers and her husband, reveal a complex web of insecurities, desires, and self-judgment, where humor acts as both a coping mechanism and a veneer for painful truths.

Through Celeste’s fixation on her appearance and her attempts at extreme dieting, McKnight critiques the unrealistic beauty standards often imposed on women. The DMV workplace, with its mundane interactions, juxtaposes the harsh reality of self-judgment with moments of camaraderie and humor. In essence, The Appetite is an exploration of identity under pressure, inviting readers to examine their own relationships with body image and to question how society shapes those perceptions.


### 

TITLE: The Damps
(c) 2006 By Nathan McKnight




Mr. Eberts drove through the night to make sure he arrived in Straitsville right at sun up. He figured he'd need most of a day's work to get the deal done, and he wanted to be out of there come nightfall. Already, he looked over his shoulder at every knock of the engine and flutter of the heart.  
*   *   *
"She's been running from that fire her whole goddamn life," said Mr. Eberts, "What makes you think she's going to stop running now?"
"Please, Bill," said Mr. Ambrose. "It would mean an awful lot. That woman practically raised me." 
Mr. Eberts finished the last crumbs of his lemon chiffon.
"Okay," he said, "but if I talk to Lizzie Green, you have to buy every last drop of Hock-Hocking I've got left."
"Hang on a sec," injected J.M., leaning over from the little window booth, "You ain't really going up to Lizzie Green's? That place ain't right, I tell ya. You're like as not to get snuffed out by mine gasses. And all that sulphur in the air--I'm telling ya--it'll eat yer eyes right outta yer head."
"This whole dang town smells like sulphur," said Mr. Eberts, "Gives me the creeps. It's why I left."
Eberts pushed his plate away.
"Don't have much choice," said Eberts. "If I don't sell this beer--and quick--I'm out of a job."
"The way I hear it, you're out of luck any case," said J.M., waving his fried egg sandwich.
"Maybe," said Eberts, "but if I unload my last few cases, I'll get my severance, and a crack at a position in the new company."
"New company?"
"The brewery's thinking of switching to soda pop come the new year."
That brought a guffaw that blew the whipped cream off J.M.'s milkshake.
"Nobody's gonna be drinkin soda pop, Mr. Eberts. If they really take away whiskey, folks around here'll just switch to mine rum."
"It's a done deal," said Eberts, turning back to Mr. Ambrose, "Prohibition is coming. So, what about us? Do we have a deal?" 
He stuck out his hand, Ambrose yanked it toward him, Eberts yanked it back, they shook high, shook low, and ended in an Indian-style "How!"
Just like old times.
"One last thing," said Mr. Eberts. 
He scribbled out a note on the back of his napkin. 
"Could I ask you to send a telegram to Hocking Brewery?" he said.
Ambrose took the napkin and read it aloud: "SALE CONFIRMED SEND THREE TRUCKS PAREN. SEVEN-FIFTY CASES CLOSE PAREN. STRAITSVILLE. A-S-A-P."
*   *   *
He could feel the breath of the ghost horses on the back window of his car as he sped up the road to Dutch Ridge. They had haunted him all his life, but the ghost hooves pounded on his trail as he fishtailed around a gravelly corner. 
He shook his head, took one hand at a time off the wheel to shake out the knucklewhite. He never should have returned to New Straitsville. 
A veil of smoke encompassed the car, and Eberts cursed. The mine fire had been burning since '94. He'd only been eleven at the time, but he way he remembered it, the Sun had died the day the striking workers started the fire. Not long afterwards, Grampa Lisander had died fighting for the town that vilified him. Family enmity burned long in these parts.
Twenty-five years later, the mine fire was still going too, spewing thick smoke from an ever-shifting landscape of fissures and sinkholes. 

When the gravel ran out, Mr. Eberts and slipped out of the lightly-worn front seat of his company car. He would miss the 1917 Pierce-Arrow.
The slam of the door wumphed softly against the low, smoky sky. Mr. Eberts held his handkerchief over his mouth and hiked an unnamed trail up to the last place he remembered Lizzie Green living. She'd never go far. Even as the ground burned, she'd refused to abandon Dutch Ridge. Just kept moving back and forth, scrounging together a new little shack each time an old one burnt down or was inundated by the damps. 
His shoes weren't made for hiking, and his slacks caught on burrs along the way through the coal-stinking woods.
After a while, he ran across the trace of the coal seam on the ground. It was a thick line, exuding the stench and heat of a coal oven, broken in places by slumping dirt. Following Dutch Ridge, he had to cross that line.
Eberts sidled up to one of the dirt slumps and dug his foot in as best he could. With teeth gritted and muscles tensed, he crossed the seam. His shoes steamed with sweat.
He kept the handkerchief tight over his mouth, but it was quickly was blotted black, despite repeated refolding. 
When he reached the top of the ridge, the Sun was a dull red glow in the black sky. The sound of galloping rushed up behind him. He turned, but nothing was there except a dark billow of smoke.
The lights of a cottage glowed through trees queerly tilted trees. He felt a quaking.
"Eeeeberrrrrts!" the shriek came from everywhere, and he knew it was the voice of Shep Tinker, coming to reclaim his partner in crime. 
The ground below began to lift. At first, he felt like the whole world was bending. He jumped and looked back. It was just one tree falling over, its enormous root system tearing out of the ground, smoking, charred.
He approached the house, watery-eyed. 
Lizzie's was a tin shack ensconced in burr oaks and a glorious old gum tree. The remains of the wagon carriage Eberts remembered the long-dead Mr. Green and the Green girls riding around town had been reduced to a weedy stack of bone-dry planks. But one wheel still held together, leaning on the gum tree. Him and Ambrose had pissed on that wheel a thousand times. But they stopped doing it after Mr. Green was taken by the mine. 

Eberts took a leak behind the gum tree for old time's sake, calming his nerves. Where his stream hit the prickly brown tree droppings, the hiss of red-hot embers hissed ammonia steam.
"Whaddaya waunt, Billy?"
Eberts fiddled with his fly and spun, dribbling on his trousers.
"Oh, uh...Lizzie Green. Well, good to see you!"
Silence burned, then Lizzie said, "Tiredest lookin smile I seen all week. Well, don't just stan there--come awn in."
Eberts sighed and followed Lizzie into the dim shack. 
"I've come to talk to you about movin," he said.
"Oh naw you don't," she said, turning right round and pushing him back onto the porch. 
"Done had enougha y'alls commin up here tryna 'talk some sense inta me.' Whatdday'alls think y'gonna say y'ain't already sed? And here's me thinkin you was just commin out to catch up with poor old Lizzie Green," she scowled. "At'n wiz on mah tree. Why should I listen to some babified city slicker, son of a horse thief, anyway?"
Mr. Eberts flushed, the sound of a thousand sets of hooves chasing him through life exploded upon his memory. 
"Now see here, Lizzie Green..." he started.
"No, you see here, boy," she said in an ancient-sounding voice. "Yer family ain't nothing but lousy good-fer-nothin's right down to the person. Shoulda got run outta town before you was even borned."
	"I've had about enough of this," shouted Mr. Eberts, stepping up to take Lizzie by the arm. 
	She backed up, trying to close the door on him, but he pushed his way in.
	"Listen here, boy..."
	"No, you listen here. I am sick of you people disrespecting my kin cause-a your stupid hillbilly legends. My grampa warn't no horse thief, and even if he was, that was a hunderd years ago! I've taken the last lip from you that I care to. You're comin with me." 

As Eberts' spoke, his eyes adjusted to the dim, and he saw the one-room house was almost entirely taken up by an improvised distillery made from a welded battery of fish kettles and the belly of an old green enamel stove. Jars piled three high filled the floor, neatly labeled: MR. BENTLEY, JACK DONLEY SR., RALPH MACKENZIE.
Eberts gaped, picked one up: EDGAR
"Why, you've got a proper nip joint here," he said.
"It wasn't the damps kep me movin," she said, "was the spirits. They died in the fire, or in fightin it. All'a them. I was the only one heard their shrieks when the ground collapsed, their whimpers when the gas overtook 'em. An' they never forgot." She shuddered. "But they tried to take me. Every night, all night, they'd jump into my body, try'na take over, failin'. It was terrible, Billy. Ah tried'ta run, I moved'ta one house after'nuther, but they always found me, and the further I went, cuss'der they got. But I finally learnt. It was the damps'at kep'em, filled the mine'n gave'em life in death. I learnt'ta capture those vapors. In the mine rum," she said, gesturing at the jar in Eberts' hand.
"You captured the mine gasses in...moonshine?"
"It's moonshine, aight, but not juss. After I distill the shine, I run it through again, purify it, and I open the kettles to let the damps in. I realized, accidentally, I silenced one. I knowed'em all by name, an Doc Able weren't swirlin bout no more. He was in the jar."
Eberts peered into the jar he held, and though it was crystal clear, he could see the barest glint of pale orange light.
He shook his head, and said, "Lizzie," steeling himself. "Lizzie, you've finally gone off your rocker."
Quick as he could, Eberts seized her around the waist and swung her bony little body over his shoulder, and turned to leave.
But he was jolted by a crash and a splash on the back of his head. He fell to his knees, his lungs filling with the sting of moonshine vapors.
As Lizzie scampered away, Mr. Eberts staggered out the door and tumbled down the porch steps. He turned with his hands protecting his face. Lizzie stood, holding a jar ready to throw. 
Delicate tendrils of vapor coalesced as they rose from Eberts' face and chest. 
He braced himself against one of the big burr oaks, and said, "Lizzie, please. I only want what's best..."
A jar burst over Eberts' head, against the tree trunk. He ducked and scampered away on all fours across the hot dirt, but fell on his face, feeling as though a powerful wind had hit him suddenly on the back. He rolled over and gaped at the burgeoning cloud of vapors luminescing fire-orange amid the ever-present smoke.
The ground was hot. His back began to burn, and he leapt to his feet. The vapors had materialized into an apparition, sweeping through the air, into his mouth, then out the back of his head. It left him simultaneously burning and icy.
Eberts gasped as another jar whirred by, thwacking his ankle before bursting into flames on the smoldering ground.
"You see what it's like now! Now you see why I gotta trap'em. The still's the only way I know how. Runnin away never worked. No matter how far you go, Billy, they're gonna chase you down."
She hefted another jar of moonshine.
He tried to stand, but only succeeded in rolling over onto his side, painful, fetal. 
The jar on the ground in front of his face was effervescing on the hot dirt. Its label said: LISANDER EBERTS. 
He reached out, forcing his hand to grab the hot jar. Ethanol steam spurted from the lid, prickling orangey-blue flames where it touched the ground.
With all his strength, he wrenched the lid, and with a puff of pressure, the jar was open.
Wavering at the maw of the jar, golden flames formed a tormented face.
"Paw?" he said. 
With all his will, he brought it to his lips for a painful-hot kiss, a swallow, a gulp, another, and another. He felt the hot alcohol surge though his gullet. He could barely breath, but with the heat, his chest tightened, his muscles tensed, hard like anthracite, and he stood, unbreathing, monolithic. 
He felt the ground rumble, and heard a single set of hooves bearing down.
"Eeeeeberrrrtsss!" 
Lizzie staggered, hand over mouth, then darted into the shack.
The burning white smoke of a ghost horse reared up and stamped its wispy hooves on the ground in front of Mr. Eberts. Riding the horse was a shriveled-looking old man.
"So, I've finally caught up with you, Mr. Eberts" said the spirit. 
Against his will, Eberts opened his mouth, and in his grandfather's voice, he said, "What of it, Shep?"
"You took my property," the mounted phantom said, "now I'm going to have to take your life."
"I had to return them to their rightful owners," said the voice from within Eberts. "But the black damp got us just as we crossed the ridge."
The ghost of Shep Tinker sidled up to Eberts, and to his shock, he stood his ground, even as Tinker placed a spectral gun against his temple.
"Say your prayers, Eberts."
At that moment, Lizzie swung the door wide, and the light of the fire under the mash pot on her still flooded the clearing. 
Tinker and Mr. Eberts both swooned. He could feel the spirit of grampa Lisander being pulled out of him. With all his might, he tried to keep the ghost inside him, hugging the spirit with his arms, his chest, his soul.  
Just before he lost his grip, he saw the spirit of Shep Tinker pulled off its mount, twisting into the cabin. It swirled above the still, fighting the inevitable. 
And there was silence.
*   *   *
The refrigerated trucks rolled into the lot, reflecting lamplight onto the gravel.
"What do you mean, you didn't talk her down?" spat Ambrose.
"Trust me, Mr. Ambrose. I've got it covered," slurred Mr. Eberts, drunk on a couple of Manhattan cocktails.
"Yeah, well, you ain't made a sale yet, so wouldn'ya say bringin all this dang beer into town is a bit hasty?"
A crowd had gathered around the trucks, and the Hock-Hocking men unloaded the cases onto Ambrose's front porch.
"Aw, sshhut up and have a bheer," said Mr. Eberts.
"But you promised," protested Mr. Ambrose.
"Shhh..." said Mr. Eberts.
The door to Ambrose Lunch Counter tinkled open.
Ambrose started, mouth agape at seeing Lizzie, dressed in her Sundays.
"Hello, Mr. Ambrose," she said, setting a jar of moonshine on the countertop. "I believe Mr. Eberts has a different proposition for you."

(c) 2024 by Nathan McKnight

His face seemed a little different too. In a good way. "Are you awake?"

"Mmmm, not quite," she said, "but talk to me. I like your voice." 

He smiled at that but then went back to the curious, almost stern, manner. "Remember you asked me something last night?"
